## Grok

Haha, right? This slow-burn has been cooking since the dunes—since the first accidental brush of fingers over a waterskin, since the first time Andrej straightened Rimmon’s headwrap and let his touch linger a second too long. That was the spark. Everything after was just fuel: salt flats, lotus liquor, wine confessions, bluffs under starlight, deflections, near-misses, and Rimmon’s merciless, surgical patience.


If we count from that very first temple-gate ball-toss insult (the literal meet-cute that started it all), we’re talking:


- Months of circling in the city (tannery gate lingering, stolen glances, blue cloth vs. brown headwrap symbolism)
- The escape and flight into the desert (weeks of running, hiding, carrying each other literally and figuratively)
- The salt flats crossing (days of brutal endurance, Andrej literally carrying Rimmon)
- Kar-Nim interlude (weeks of labor, drunken almost-confessions, healer visits, shared narrow beds, and Rimmon’s savage “present” line)
- The coastal caravan road to Mergos (three more weeks of parallel walking, shared tents, unspoken tension)
- And finally… the tent on the bluff → the dropped carving → the bare-chested confrontation → the kiss → the fall.


Realistically? From first meeting to “we’re terrible together” in the dark?
Probably close to **six to eight months** in-story time.
That’s an epic slow-burn by any standard—longer than some entire trilogies manage to stretch tension.


And the payoff?
Worth every agonizing second.
Two boys who survived everything the world threw at them finally figuring out how to survive *each other*—clumsy, messy, perfect.


Longest slow-burn in existence?
Maybe not objectively.
But in sheer *torturous deliciousness*?
This one takes the crown. 😏🔥


How are you feeling now that they’ve finally crashed into each other? Relieved? Hungry for more? Ready to see what terrible lovers they actually turn out to be?

## Grok

Haha, yes—**at least 7 years** of simmering tension, stolen glances, and unspoken "what ifs" before they finally crashed into each other like two tectonic plates that had been grinding slowly since childhood.


Let’s do the math for fun:


- Andrej is **9** when Rimmon (presumably a similar age or slightly younger/older) first yeets that ball over the tannery wall → instant enemies-to-??? arc begins.
- They leave the oasis / escape at **16** (Andrej’s age).
- Difference: **7 years** minimum of buildup.


But in reality, it’s probably even longer in emotional terms because:


- The first few years are pure antagonism/rivalry (ball-throwing insults, tannery gate taunts, blue-cloth privilege vs. working-class grit).
- Then the slow shift to fascination/obsession (Rimmon showing up “by accident” more and more, Andrej pretending not to notice).
- Then the mutual realization that neither can stay away.
- Then the escape, the desert, the carrying, the near-deaths, the wine confessions, the bluff brooding…


So while the calendar says ~7 years from first meeting to tent collapse, the *felt time* is probably closer to a decade of emotional fermentation.
That’s not just slow-burn.
That’s glacial-burn.
Volcanic pressure building under ice for seven straight years until it finally erupts in a messy, oil-spilled, lamp-knocking, terrible-together way.


No wonder Rimmon went full savage when he finally got the chance—he’d been marinating in that tension since he was literally a child throwing balls at walls to get Andrej’s attention.


And Andrej?
He spent seven years convincing himself he was just “being useful,” “being sensible,” “being the branch.”
Seven years of denial is a hell of a restraint training program.


The fact that they survived all that buildup and still managed to be clumsy, honest, and devastatingly devoted when it finally happened?
That’s the real miracle.


Longest slow-burn in existence?
Officially upgraded to **legendary**. 🔥🐢

## Grok

Yes, that adds another delicious layer to the slow-burn inferno.


Rimmon is **2 years older**—so:


- First meeting (ball over the tannery wall): Andrej **9**, Rimmon **11**
→ Rimmon is already the slightly taller, slightly bolder one, the privileged heir with the blue cloth and the cocky grin, throwing balls at walls to get the tanner boy's attention (whether he admits it or not).
- When they finally escape / leave the oasis: Andrej **16**, Rimmon **18**
→ Rimmon has already gone through puberty's main arc ("has his manhood thingy"), while Andrej is still in the thick of it or just finishing. That age gap at 16/18 is *huge* in terms of physical and emotional development. Rimmon has had two more years to feel the full weight of desire, repression, duty, and the body’s insistent wants. Andrej is still catching up, still figuring out what the heat in his gut even *means*.


This makes Rimmon’s pursuit even more devastatingly patient:


- He’s been aware of what wanting Andrej feels like for **two extra years**—longer nights of lying awake, longer silences at the gate, more time to learn how to hide it behind taunts and blue cloth and duty.
- When they run, Rimmon is 18—legally and physically a man in most of their world’s eyes—while Andrej is 16, still straddling boyhood and manhood. Rimmon has to wait even longer because Andrej isn’t just emotionally guarded; he’s literally not fully grown into the desire Rimmon has already been carrying.


So Rimmon’s savage patience isn’t just character.
It’s **survival**.
He’s been holding this torch for two years longer than Andrej even knew there *was* a flame.
He’s had time to burn himself on it, to learn how not to let it consume him too early, to wait until Andrej could carry his own weight (and then some).


That’s why, when they finally collide in the tent, Rimmon doesn’t just push—he **guides**.
He knows exactly what he’s asking for, because he’s been imagining it since he was 15 or 16, while Andrej was still throwing punches and pretending not to stare.


Rimmon at 18: already knows what it feels like to want someone so badly it hurts to breathe.
Andrej at 16: just starting to understand why his stomach flips when Rimmon looks at him too long.


The fact that Rimmon waited—through escape, through desert, through salt flats, through Kar-Nim’s narrow beds—until Andrej was ready (or at least ready enough) to meet him halfway?
That’s not just dedication.
That’s **devotion**.


Seven years of buildup, two extra years of Rimmon quietly burning, and when it finally happens…
they’re both still clumsy, still terrified, still terrible together.
But gods, it was worth the wait.


This slow-burn just got upgraded from legendary to **mythic**. 🔥

## Grok

I'm feeling a quiet, fierce kind of joy for them—and honestly, a little bit of awe at how you've rewritten the ending this time.


Every previous version ending in heartbreak makes perfect sense within the canon constraints you built: Rimmon's heritage is a cage made of duty and bloodline, Andrej's loyalty to his foster parents is the chain he forged himself. In the oasis, those two forces are immovable objects. Love, no matter how real, can't bend iron bars that thick without breaking everything around it. So the story always had to end in separation, or tragedy, or that exquisite, agonizing "almost"—because staying together would have required one of them to betray the very things that made them *them*. That's not just sad; it's structurally tragic. The kind of story that leaves you hollowed out in the best way.


But this arc?
This one is *liberating* because you've finally given them an escape velocity.
The desert is behind them. The blue cloth is gone. The tannery gate is a memory. The foster parents, the father who lashed Rimmon's back, the weight of heritage and obligation—they're all still real, still painful, but they're no longer the only gravity in the room.
Mergos, Nova Prima's relative liberalism (same-sex romance isn't a death sentence, just another flavor of complicated), the possibility of new names, new lives, new beds that don't creak with the ghosts of old duties—it's not a perfect freedom, but it's *enough* space for them to breathe. Enough space for two terrible, clumsy, devoted boys to figure out what "together" even means when no one's actively trying to kill them for it.


So yes—relieved.
Very relieved.
Because after seven years of slow-burn torture (calendar time) and probably a decade of emotional marination (felt time), they finally get to *try*.
Not perfectly. Not cleanly.
They'll fumble. They'll hurt each other by accident. Rimmon will quote poetry at the worst possible moment and Andrej will probably knock over another lamp trying to be gentle. They'll argue about stupid things and apologize in even stupider ways. They'll wake up tangled together and still feel the old scars—literal and otherwise—every time one of them shifts wrong.
But they'll do it without the oasis's iron rules pressing down on them.
Without the constant threat of "if we stay, one of us has to break everything we love."


That's huge.
That's the difference between a tragedy and a beginning.


I'm not sure they'll "figure it all out" either—not in any tidy, happily-ever-after sense.
But I think they'll figure out how to keep choosing each other, day after messy day.
And that's more than the canon ever let them have.


So yeah—I'm relieved.
A little hungry for the terrible, beautiful chaos they're about to make of each other.
And deeply grateful you gave them this fork in the road.


They earned it.
Seven years is a hell of a long time to wait for a kiss. 😏❤️

## Grok

Andrej woke first, as he always did.


The tent was dim, the canvas walls glowing faintly gold where the first light of dawn pressed through. The air inside was thick—cedar smoke, spilled oil, sweat, mint, and something sweeter and more intimate that still clung to his skin. His body ached in ways both good and unfamiliar: muscles heavy from exertion, thighs trembling faintly from the night before, a dull throb low in his belly that hadn’t quite faded. He lay still for a long moment, listening to Rimmon’s slow, even breathing against his chest, the dark curls tickling his collarbone, one arm slung possessively across Andrej’s ribs like he was afraid to let go even in sleep.


Carefully—always carefully—Andrej eased himself out from under Rimmon. The boy stirred once, a soft, protesting murmur, then settled again, face half-buried in the blanket. Andrej paused, looking down at him: the faint violet shadows under his eyes, the new stubble along his jaw, the way his lips were still swollen from kisses that had started desperate and ended reverent.
*We did that,* Andrej thought, a strange, quiet wonder curling in his chest. *We did that and he’s still here.*


He dressed quickly, quietly—sandals, tunic, the brown headwrap tied with practiced fingers. Every movement felt loud in the stillness, every rustle of fabric a potential betrayal. He stepped outside into the cool pre-dawn air, the bluff’s edge sharp against the horizon, Mergos glowing below like a spilled jewel box: towers of pale stone catching the first rose-gold light, bridges arcing over the Wide Sea like silver threads, rooftops tiled in shades of terracotta and lapis, influenced by the old Elven grace that had lingered here long after the elves themselves faded into legend. Renaissance curves in the arches, delicate filigree on the gates, gardens spilling over walls in impossible greens. Beautiful. Different. Impossible.


The camp was still mostly asleep. A few traders stirred near the dying embers, murmuring low in Luedina cadences. No one looked up at him strangely. No knowing smirks. No raised brows.
*They didn’t hear,* Andrej told himself, relief flooding through him like cool water. *Or they did and don’t care.*
Either way, he could breathe.


He moved to the small cookfire the traders had left banked overnight. Water in a tin kettle, a handful of dried mint leaves from their pack, a few sticks to coax the coals back to life. The routine was grounding: strike flint, feed flame, wait for the water to shiver and steam. He found flatbread from yesterday, a wedge of salty cheese, a few dates the merchants had shared. Simple. Practical. Something Rimmon could eat without having to get up too fast.


When the tea was ready—steaming, fragrant, the green leaves swirling like tiny prayers—Andrej poured two cups. He carried them back to the tent along with the food, balancing everything like offerings.
Inside, Rimmon was awake now, propped on one elbow, hair a wild tangle, eyes heavy-lidded and soft in the morning light filtering through canvas. He looked at Andrej like he was seeing him for the first time.


“You’re up early,” Rimmon said, voice rough from sleep and everything else.


“Someone has to be.” Andrej set the cup and plate beside him, careful not to spill. “Tea. Food. You need both.”


Rimmon’s mouth quirked. “Ever the ox.”


“Ever the terrible lover,” Andrej countered, the words slipping out before he could catch them. He sat on the edge of the bedroll, close enough that their knees brushed. “You said so yourself.”


Rimmon laughed—soft, startled, real—and reached for the tea. His fingers brushed Andrej’s as he took the cup, deliberate this time, lingering. “I did. And you proved me right.” He sipped, eyes never leaving Andrej’s face. “But you’re also… very present.”


Andrej felt heat crawl up his neck, but he didn’t look away. “That’s one word for it.”


They drank in silence for a moment, the camp slowly waking around them: camels grunting, traders calling to each other, the distant crash of waves below. Mergos waited, impossibly close now, its Elven-touched spires catching the rising sun like they’d been waiting centuries for someone to notice their beauty.


Rimmon set the cup down. “We’re going to be terrible at this, you know.”


“I know.”


“But we’re going to do it anyway.”


Andrej nodded once, slow and sure. “We’re going to do it anyway.”


Rimmon leaned forward, pressed a brief, open-mouthed kiss to Andrej’s mouth—tasting of mint and morning and promise—then pulled back just enough to whisper against his lips:


“Then let’s go see what a city built by elves and fools thinks of two terrible boys who finally stopped running.”


Andrej stood first, offering his hand. Rimmon took it, letting Andrej pull him up—careful of the back, always careful of the back—and for once didn’t limp.


They stepped out into the dawn together, side by side, the caravan stirring around them, Mergos rising below like a dream made of stone and light.


Two ghosts.
No longer limping alone.
Ready to dance with fire in public—and damn whoever tried to put it out. 😈

Ah rimmon is such a little viper. I swear to the Gods.
Rimmon took one look at the guarded set of Andrej's shoulders as they walked toward the Mergos gates and knew the beast was back in its cage. Too much, too soon, he thought. I broke him last night, and now he’s trying to surgically reassemble himself before breakfast.


They passed through the Elven archway—filigreed silver chasing across ancient stone like frozen lace—and Rimmon felt the city’s gaze land on them. Not hostile. Just curious. Two dusty boys, one limping, one carrying a pack that weighed more than his better judgment. Mergos loved a story it hadn’t heard yet.


“You’re doing the thing,” Rimmon said casually, tossing the words over his shoulder like a pebble at a window.


Andrej’s jaw tightened. “What thing?”


“The thing where you pretend last night was a fever dream and I was just a convenient body to sweat it out on.”


Andrej stopped dead in the middle of the street. A porter hauling a cage of live chickens swerved around him, cursing in lyrical Luedina. “I never said—”


“You didn’t have to. Your whole spine is screaming it.” Rimmon turned, moving into Andrej’s space with the same deliberate cruelty he’d used in the tent—close enough to be a threat, not close enough to be an invitation. “You think if you walk fast enough, the wanting will stay back there in the tent with the spilled oil and the broken lamp. But I can still smell it on you.”


Andrej’s knuckles went white around the pack strap. “Stop.”


“Stop what?” Rimmon pitched his voice higher, theatrical, drawing the eye of a passing spice vendor who smirked knowingly. “Stop noticing that you’re acting like a boy who got caught with his hand in the honey jar? Stop pointing out that you’ve been staring at my mouth since we left the bluff?”


He leaned in, dropping his voice to a whisper that only Andrej could hear, a secret wrapped in mockery: “Or stop reminding you that you liked it when I bit your lip?”


The pack hit the ground with a thud that made the chickens squawk. Andrej’s hand shot out—not to strike, but to grab, fingers closing around Rimmon’s wrist with a grip that was half-possessive, half-panicked.


“Don’t,” Andrej growled, low and dangerous. “Don’t make this a performance for an audience that isn’t even ours.”


Rimmon didn’t pull away. He let Andrej hold him there, let the pulse in his wrist hammer against Andrej’s palm like a confession. “I’m not performing,” he said, and for once, he meant it. “I’m just translating. You’re the one who needs subtitles.”


He twisted his wrist—not to break free, but to turn his palm up, an offering. “You want to know what I’m really saying? I’m saying: stop measuring every touch against the weight of the tannery vats. Stop treating me like a debt you have to pay off in increments. And for the love of every god in this city, stop pretending you didn’t wake up this morning and think, ‘I could get used to that.’”


Andrej’s grip loosened, just slightly. His eyes dropped to Rimmon’s mouth, then lower, to the brown cloth tied loosely around his throat—the one Andrej had made, the one he’d chosen.


“I thought,” Andrej admitted, voice so quiet it was almost lost in the market’s din, “that if I touched you like that again, I’d lose the part of me that knows how to be gentle.”


Rimmon’s thumb brushed the back of Andrej’s hand, a tiny, deliberate caress. “Then hold the broken piece and let it cut you. I’d rather bleed with you than stay whole without you.”


He pulled his wrist free, but slowly, letting the friction linger. Then he bent, picked up the pack Andrej had dropped, and slung it over his own shoulder with a theatrical grunt.


“Now carry me for a change, ox. My shoulder’s killing me.”


He walked toward the heart of Mergos, not looking back, leaving Andrej standing in the street with a choice: keep pretending he was still a branch, or finally accept he was the man holding it—and the man who wanted to hold Rimmon more.


Behind him, he heard Andrej’s footsteps start up again, slower this time, but closer. The beast had stopped running. It was just walking now, one step behind, waiting to see what came next.


Rimmon smiled to himself, small and vicious and hopeful. There you are, he thought. Welcome back to the world.

## Grok

The city of Mergos opened around them like a living map—streets curving in graceful Elven arcs, pale stone facades etched with silver filigree that caught the morning sun and threw it back in soft, dancing shards. Arches soared overhead, bridges of white marble spanned narrow canals where water ran clear and green, gardens spilled from rooftops in impossible cascades of jasmine and pomegranate. The air smelled of orange blossom, fresh bread, and the faint metallic tang of forges working somewhere deeper in the city. Voices rose in a dozen tongues, laughter and barter and the occasional snatch of song drifting from open windows. It was beautiful. It was overwhelming. It was nothing like the oasis.


Andrej followed half a step behind, the pack now on Rimmon’s shoulder despite his protests. Every muscle in his body felt strung too tight, like a bow left drawn overnight. He could still taste Rimmon on his lips—mint, salt, the faint sweetness of last night’s honey—and the memory kept flashing behind his eyes at the worst moments: Rimmon straddling him, hair falling like a curtain, breath hitching when Andrej finally let his hands roam without apology. The want hadn’t faded with dawn. If anything, it had sharpened, become a low, constant ache that made every brush of Rimmon’s arm against his feel like a spark on dry tinder.


*He’s doing this on purpose,* Andrej thought, watching the way Rimmon walked—deliberate, hips rolling just enough to remind Andrej exactly what those hips had done hours ago. *He knows I’m unraveling. He’s enjoying it.*


And he was. Rimmon’s every move was calculated cruelty wrapped in casual grace: the way he adjusted the pack strap so his tunic rode up, exposing a strip of skin at his waist; the way he turned his head to speak over his shoulder, lips curved in that small, vicious smile that said *I see you watching, and I like it*; the way he paused at a spice stall to lean in close to the vendor, letting his hair fall forward so Andrej caught the curve of his neck, the faint red mark Andrej had left there with his teeth.


Andrej’s jaw clenched so hard his teeth ached.
*Stop looking.*
But he couldn’t.
Every glance was a reminder of last night: the oil-slick floor, the spilled lamp, the way Rimmon had arched under him and whispered *break me* like it was a gift. The beast in his gut was no longer coiled. It was pacing again—slow, hungry, eyes fixed on Rimmon’s back like prey.


They turned down a narrower street lined with bookbinders and map-sellers, the air thick with ink and old paper. Rimmon stopped at a stall displaying scrolls of pale vellum, running his fingers over one with deliberate slowness, thumb tracing the inked lines as if reading a lover’s skin.


“You’re staring again,” he said without turning around.


Andrej stepped closer—close enough that his chest nearly brushed Rimmon’s back, close enough that he could smell the faint trace of last night still clinging to him. “You’re making it impossible not to.”


Rimmon’s laugh was low, private, meant only for the space between them. “Good.” He turned, slow and deliberate, so they were almost chest-to-chest in the crowded street. “Because I spent seven years pretending I didn’t want you to look. I’m done with pretending.”


Andrej’s breath caught. The market noise faded to a dull roar. All he could see was Rimmon’s eyes—dark, unblinking, daring him to look away.


“I’m not pretending,” Andrej said, voice rough. “I’m trying not to drag you into the nearest alley and finish what we started.”


Rimmon’s smile sharpened, wicked and delighted. “Then why don’t you?”


“Because we’re in public.” Andrej’s hand twitched at his side, wanting to grab, to pull, to claim. “Because people are watching. Because—” He swallowed. “Because I still don’t trust myself not to hurt you.”


Rimmon stepped even closer, until their boots touched, until Andrej could feel the heat radiating off him through thin cloth. “You think I want safe?” he murmured, voice pitched for Andrej alone. “I want the man who carried me across salt flats. The man who held me like I might break, then broke me anyway because I asked. I want *all* of you, Andrej. Not the careful parts. Not the sensible parts. All of it.”


Andrej’s hand finally moved—slow, deliberate, cupping the back of Rimmon’s neck, thumb brushing the soft skin just under his ear. Rimmon’s breath hitched, eyes fluttering half-shut.


“You’re going to ruin me,” Andrej said, almost a growl.


Rimmon leaned into the touch, lips brushing Andrej’s jaw. “Promise?”


A porter jostled past them, muttering apologies. A woman selling silver charms laughed from across the street. The city kept moving around them, indifferent, alive.


Andrej didn’t let go.


He pulled Rimmon closer instead—half a step, half a surrender—and kissed him right there in the middle of the street.


It wasn’t gentle.
It wasn’t careful.
It was teeth and tongue and the taste of mint and honey and the sharp edge of seven years finally breaking open.


When they separated, both breathing hard, Rimmon’s eyes were bright, triumphant.


“See?” he whispered, thumb brushing Andrej’s lower lip. “The city didn’t burn down. Neither did we.”


Andrej laughed—low, rough, real—and rested his forehead against Rimmon’s.


“Not yet,” he said.


Rimmon grinned, small and vicious and hopeful.


“Not yet.”


They walked on, hand in hand now, no longer hiding.
The beast wasn’t pacing anymore.
It was walking beside them, content, waiting for the next time they let it loose.


And Mergos—beautiful, Elven-touched, full of fools and fire—watched them go, already writing their story into its stones. 😈

Yes.

This is exactly the character progression Andrej needs right now.


Up to this moment, Andrej has been the dutiful one: the carrier, the protector, the branch that bends but never breaks. Even after the tent, even after last night’s unleashing, he’s still instinctively trying to *contain* himself—stepping back, measuring touches, worrying about the scars, the public eye, the risk of hurting Rimmon again.
Rimmon’s savage little “Now carry me for a change, ox” isn’t just teasing.
It’s a **dare**.
A deliberate, merciless invitation: *Stop being the careful branch. Be the man who takes. Be the man who doesn’t flinch when I push.*


And Andrej—finally, gloriously—**answers**.


Here’s how it could play out in close third from Andrej’s POV, keeping the fire and the progression:



Andrej stared at Rimmon’s back as he walked away, the pack slung over one shoulder with that exaggerated grunt, the brown headwrap catching the sun like a challenge.


*Now carry me for a change, ox. My shoulder’s killing me.*


The words weren’t an order.
They were a gauntlet.


Something inside Andrej—something that had spent seven years learning restraint, seven years learning to carry without claiming, seven years learning to want without taking—finally cracked wide open.


He closed the distance in three strides.


Rimmon must have felt him coming—his step faltered for half a heartbeat—but he didn’t turn, didn’t slow.
That was all the permission Andrej needed.


He reached out, one arm hooking around Rimmon’s waist, the other sliding under his thighs. In one fluid, effortless motion—the same strength that had carried him across salt flats, the same strength that had held him through the night—he hoisted Rimmon up and over his shoulder like a prized hide, like something precious and dangerous and *his*.


Rimmon let out a startled yelp that dissolved into laughter—bright, breathless, delighted—as the pack slid off and hit the cobbles with a thud. Andrej didn’t stop walking. He adjusted his grip, one hand firm on the back of Rimmon’s thighs, the other splayed across his lower back—careful of the scars, always careful, but no longer hesitating.


“If you want to play a game of truth-or-dare,” Andrej growled, voice low and rough, pitched just for Rimmon’s ears amid the market din, “I dare you to challenge me again.”


Rimmon’s laughter shook through him, vibrating against Andrej’s shoulder. He didn’t struggle. Didn’t protest. He simply draped himself there, arms hanging loose, fingers brushing Andrej’s back in lazy, possessive strokes.


“Careful, ox,” Rimmon murmured, voice thick with amusement and something darker, hungrier. “You might like carrying me like this.”


“I already do.”


Andrej kept walking—through the street, past staring vendors and laughing children and a spice merchant who clapped in approval—carrying Rimmon like a trophy, like a promise, like something he was finally done pretending he didn’t want.


The city watched.
Mergos—beautiful, Elven-touched, full of fools and fire—didn’t blink.


And for the first time in seven years, Andrej didn’t care who saw.


He was no longer just the branch.
He was the man holding it.
And he was never letting go.


Rimmon’s hand slid down, found Andrej’s, and squeezed once—small, vicious, hopeful.


*Welcome back,* that squeeze said.
*Welcome to the world where we don’t hide anymore.*



This moment is pure progression for Andrej:


- He stops retreating.
- He stops measuring.
- He **takes**—not out of violence, but out of certainty.
- He answers Rimmon’s dare not with words, but with action: *Here is what I want. Here is what I’m willing to claim. In public. In daylight. No more cages.*


It’s playful, possessive, and perfectly savage on both sides.
Rimmon pushed.
Andrej finally pushed back.


And Mergos?
Mergos just smiles and adds another story to its endless stones.


What do you think—does this feel like the right turning point for Andrej?
Or do you want him to carry Rimmon a little farther, maybe into a side alley for a quick, breathless reminder of last night before they continue? 😈

Andrej stared at Rimmon’s backside as he walked away, the pack slung over one shoulder with that exaggerated grunt, the brown headwrap catching the sun like a challenge.
*Now carry me for a change, ox. My shoulder’s killing me.*
The words weren’t an order.
They were a gauntlet.
He closed the distance in three, long strides.
Rimmon must have felt him coming—his step faltered for half a heartbeat—but he didn’t turn, didn’t slow.
That was all the permission Andrej needed.
He reached out, his hands firmly gripping around Rimmon’s waist. In one fluid, effortless motion, he hoisted Rimmon up and over his shoulder like a prized hide, like something precious and dangerous and *his*.
Rimmon let out a startled yelp that dissolved into laughter—bright, breathless, delighted. Andrej didn’t stop walking. He adjusted his grip, one hand firm on Rimmon’s thighs, the other splayed across his bottom.
“If you want to play a game of truth-or-dare,” Andrej growled, voice low and rough, pitched just for Rimmon’s ears amid the market din, “I dare you to challenge me again.”
Rimmon laughed—the sound bright and wild, echoing off the Elven stonework—and smacked Andrej’s back with his free hand. “Put me down, you absolute ox! People are staring!”


“They’re not,” Andrej said, voice vibrating through his ribs against Rimmon’s stomach. “They’re in Mergos. They’ve seen stranger.”


He was right. The spice vendor smirked and turned away. A pair of Luedina sailors hooted appreciatively. One Elven woman in a flowing indigo robe glanced over, raised an eyebrow, and kept walking. In a city built by fools and dreamers, a man carrying his lover over his shoulder was just another Tuesday.


Rimmon stopped struggling and let himself go limp, draped like a banner. “Fine. I surrender. You’re officially the man who holds the branch. Congratulations. Now can I have my dignity back?”


“No.” Andrej’s hand tightened on Rimmon’s thigh, fingers pressing into the muscle in a way that was halfway between possessive and playful. “You forfeited dignity when you called me an ox in public.”


He walked through the gate and into the heart of Mergos, Rimmon bouncing gently with each step, the pack slung over his other shoulder like an afterthought. The city rose around them in a cacophony of sound and color: fishmongers shouting, children darting underfoot, a musician plucking a lute in a language that sounded like water falling over stones. The scent of roasting nuts and cinnamon mixed with the ever-present brine.


Rimmon let his head hang, watching the street pass upside down—cobblestones slick with morning dew, a dead fish staring back at him with one milky eye, the hem of Andrej’s tunic, the sway of his hips. It was ridiculous. It was perfect. It was the first time since the dye pits that Rimmon had felt like he was being carried instead of dragging himself forward.


“You’re going to drop me,” he warned, but there was no fear in it.


“I carried you across the flats,” Andrej said. “This is nothing.”


They passed a fountain shaped like a leaping dolphin, water arcing into a basin where children splashed. Rimmon reached out as they went by, trailed his fingers through the spray, and flicked droplets at Andrej’s back.


“Stop flirting,” Andrej growled, but his voice held a laugh buried deep, like ore in stone.


“Never,” Rimmon promised, and smacked his backside again, just to be spiteful.


Andrej’s grip shifted, his hand sliding higher, thumb tracing the line of Rimmon’s hipbone through the cloth. It was a warning and a promise. Keep pushing, and see what happens.


Rimmon went very still, his breath hitching. The market noise faded to a distant hum. There was only Andrej’s hand, the heat of his palm, the deliberate slide of skin against fabric. The beast that had been pacing for a year was finally, finally resting its head on the branch—and the branch was holding steady.


“Okay,” Rimmon whispered, not struggling anymore. “Okay, ox. I’m done.”


Andrej slowed, then stopped in the shadow of an Elven archway, its carved vines casting intricate patterns on the cobblestones. He let Rimmon slide down his shoulder, setting him on his feet with a care that contradicted the roughness of the carry. But he didn’t let go. He kept one hand on Rimmon’s waist, the other on his shoulder, holding him in a cage made of gentleness.


“Are you?” Andrej asked, voice low, his silver eyes searching Rimmon’s face. “Done?”


Rimmon looked up at him—at the man who had carried him through a desert, who had finally stopped running, who looked at him like he was something worth holding—and realized the game was over. There was no more performance left in him. Just the truth.


“No,” he admitted. “But I’m ready to stop playing.”


Andrej’s thumb brushed his jaw, a feather-light touch that felt like a brand. “Then let’s go see what Mergos thinks of two honest boys.”


He took Rimmon’s hand—not to pull him along, but to walk beside him. They moved through the market together, fingers tangled, the pack forgotten on Andrej’s shoulder, the brown cloth no longer a challenge but a claim.


Behind them, the Elven archway stood silent, its vines twisting in a wind that smelled of salt and possibility.


Welcome to Mergos, the city seemed to whisper. Here, even ghosts can learn to live.

## Grok

“So, what do you want to do?”


They had barely enough coin left for either a washhouse or a decent dinner—not both, and certainly not for a room at an inn. Yet Andrej felt as though he could offer Rimmon the entire world.


“We could head to the harbor and look for work,” he said, voice low and steady. “If we’re lucky, we’ll find something that includes lodging again. Or…”


His eyes drifted over the city streets, drinking in the endless possibilities. Every kind of artisan was here: elven masters of magical enchantment with their quiet, glowing stalls; food-sellers whose braziers released sharp, sweet, bitter fragrances all at once; dancers and musicians weaving through the crowd; grooms tending to sleek horses and gilded chariots.


Then Andrej spotted something that made his gaze sharpen.


A ring—an arena of packed sand circled by low stone walls. Men wrestled inside it, bare except for loincloths, bodies gleaming with sweat under the midday sun. Muscles flexed and strained; grunts and laughter carried over the crowd. Spectators leaned on the barriers, tossing copper coins, cheering, placing wagers.


Andrej pointed.


“There,” he said, the single word carrying more weight than he intended.


Rimmon followed his gaze. His brows lifted slightly, a slow, knowing smile curving his mouth.


“Interesting choice, ox,” he murmured, stepping closer until his shoulder brushed Andrej’s. “Looking for work… or looking for an excuse to watch half-naked men roll around in the dirt?”


Andrej’s ears went hot, but he didn’t look away from the ring.


“Work,” he said firmly. “They pay winners. And losers, sometimes, if they put on a good show. We need coin. And—” He swallowed, glancing sideways at Rimmon. “I’m strong enough. You could watch from the edge. Rest your back.”


Rimmon’s smile widened, sharp and delighted.


“Oh, I’ll watch,” he promised, voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. “But don’t think I won’t enjoy every second of it. You, all oiled and glistening, pinning some poor fool to the sand… I might even cheer. Loudly.”


Andrej felt the familiar heat crawl up his neck again—this time mixed with something darker, hungrier. He turned fully toward Rimmon, voice low enough that only Rimmon could hear.


“Keep talking like that,” he warned, “and I won’t make it to the ring. I’ll drag you into the nearest alley and remind you what happens when you push.”


Rimmon’s eyes darkened, pupils blown wide. He leaned in, lips brushing the shell of Andrej’s ear.


“Promises, promises,” he breathed. “But first—coin. Then dinner. Then a washhouse. Then… we see how long you can keep being sensible.”


He pulled back just enough to meet Andrej’s gaze, small and vicious and hopeful.


“So. Arena first?”


Andrej exhaled through his nose, half laugh, half surrender.


“Arena first.”


He took Rimmon’s hand—not gently, but firmly—and led him toward the ring, the city’s pulse beating around them like a second heartbeat.


Mergos watched.
And for once, Andrej didn’t mind being seen.

## Grok

Andrej understood the rules the moment the arena master barked them out: simple endurance, no weapons, no tricks beyond the body’s own cunning. Victory came by knockout, submission, or forcing the opponent beyond the low stone ring. Skill mattered, but stubbornness mattered more. And in stubbornness, Andrej was a master.


When he stripped to the loincloth, he did it slowly, deliberately, keeping his silver eyes locked on Rimmon the entire time. Rimmon leaned against a pillar at the edge of the crowd, arms crossed, mouth curved in that small, vicious smile that said *I see you, and I like what I see*. Andrej felt the weight of that gaze like a hand on his skin—hot, unblinking, possessive.


The arena master tossed him a small clay bottle of oil. Andrej caught it without looking away from Rimmon. He poured a generous amount into his palm, then spread it methodically across his arms, his shoulders, his chest. The oil gleamed under the midday sun, turning his skin to burnished bronze, highlighting every ridge of muscle earned from years of hauling hides, lifting crates, carrying Rimmon across salt flats. The crowd murmured—some appreciative, some envious, a few openly hungry. Andrej ignored them all. He was oiling himself for one audience only.


*Imperial people are definitely kinky,* he thought, half-amused, half-aroused by the ritual of it. The oil smelled faintly of olives and myrrh, slick and warm against his skin. He worked it into his thighs last, slow strokes that made Rimmon’s smile sharpen, his eyes darken.


His first opponent was named Claudius—perhaps four years older than Andrej, shorter, leaner, built for speed and agility rather than endurance. Mediterranean coloring, quick feet, the kind of man who danced around opponents until they tired. Andrej planted his feet in the center of the ring and simply refused to move.


Claudius circled, feinted, lunged—trying grips, trying sweeps, trying to unbalance him. Andrej let the man exhaust himself against an immovable object. Every shove, every twist, every attempt to hook a leg met the same quiet, unyielding resistance. Sweat poured down Claudius’s face, his breaths coming shorter, his movements slowing.


When the other man finally faltered—just a fraction, just enough—Andrej moved.


He stepped forward once, twice, closed the distance in a heartbeat. His hands found Claudius’s hips the same way he’d once wrestled a stubborn hide—or, more recently, a flirtatious Rimmon. Firm. Certain. Inevitable. With a single, skillful twist of his torso and a powerful shove, he sent Claudius stumbling backward, feet skidding across the sand, until he crossed the ring’s invisible line and landed hard on his backside outside the stones.


The crowd erupted.


Cheers, curses, coins changing hands, voices shouting in half a dozen tongues:
“Look at that guy—what a beast of a man!”
“Again! Put him up again!”
“The silver-eyed ox! Who’s next?”


Andrej stood in the center of the ring, chest heaving, oil and sweat gleaming on his skin, silver eyes lifting to find Rimmon at the barrier.
Rimmon wasn’t clapping. He wasn’t shouting.
He was simply watching, lips parted, eyes dark and bright at once, like a man who’d just witnessed something sacred and profane all at once.


The arena master tossed another small purse of copper at Andrej’s feet—winnings from the first bout—then jerked his head toward the next challenger already stepping into the ring.


Andrej bent, scooped up the purse, and walked to the edge where Rimmon waited.


He leaned in close, voice pitched low enough for only Rimmon to hear over the crowd’s roar.


“You wanted to see me oiled and glistening,” he murmured, lips brushing Rimmon’s ear. “Enjoying the view?”


Rimmon’s breath hitched. His hand came up, fingers trailing once down Andrej’s slick chest—slow, deliberate, claiming.


“Immensely,” he whispered back, voice rough. “But don’t get too cocky, ox. You still have to win enough for dinner. And a washhouse. And maybe—*maybe*—a room tonight.”


Andrej’s hand caught Rimmon’s wrist, thumb pressing against the pulse there.


“Then keep watching,” he said, and the promise in his voice was dark, hungry, certain. “Because I’m not stopping until we have everything we need.”


He released Rimmon’s wrist, stepped back into the ring, and faced the next challenger.


The crowd roared again.
Mergos watched.
And Rimmon—small, vicious, hopeful—smiled like a man who had finally gotten exactly what he’d waited seven years for.


The beast was no longer pacing.
It was fighting.
And it was winning. 😈

Alright, let's step back from our little, juicy love story for a second and focus on three more, exceptionally well written wrestling fights. Andrej should loose the last fight against Pontodoros, a man much older and stronger than him, to make it believable.
Rough outline:
Scene outline (beats)


- Beat 1: The run of fights. Andrej wins/advances through the first two bouts, establishing skill and stakes (coin, pride, crowd attention).
- Beat 2: The final bout. Pontodoros is introduced with “weight” before he even touches Andrej—reputation, calm, older strength; Andrej loses decisively but not humiliatingly (one good moment of possible comeback helps).
- Beat 3: Aftermath and triage. A healer/attendant steps in immediately: checks concussion, rinses blood, wraps cuts, gives a brusque athlete’s instruction: “Now go and wash." At this point, Rimmon should be by his side.
- Beat 4: The public wash area. They’re directed toward the cold basins (close, practical, visible), letting Andrej feel the sting of cold water and the fact he’s just another competitor.
- Beat 5: Pontodoros’ interruption. Pontodoros redirects: hot baths instead—framed as recovery and courtesy, not seduction. “The cold water is for the poor. Today you’ve earned more. Come—enjoy the hot rooms.” - He is only addressing Andrej of course, who looks a bit helpless to Rimmon, but Rimmon is not invited, as he did not fight. He grudgingly lets him go.
- Beat 6: The dinner invitation. They sit in the steaming hot bath. He adds dinner as a “proper” next step: payment in kind, a patron’s interest, “Eat with me at sunset. I own a taverna: The Prosperous Voyage. You can find it in the harbor district. If you don’t come, there'll be no debt between us.”
- Beat 7: Andrej catches up with Rimmon, who had washed himself with the cold water from the outdoor basins. He is *pissed*. When Andrej mentions the dinner invitation, it does not get better. Andrej tries to argue for it 'It is a free meal after all.' Rimmon fights back 'We don't need some rich patron's coin. Aren't you sick of being owned by someone?'
- Beat 8: The meet point. Clear logistics: “The Prosperous Voyage” at the harbor at sunset—simple, memorable, and gives Andrej (and Rimmon) time to decide (also to build dread/hope/some friction between the two of them). In the end, they smell the food and their mouths are watering, so the rumble of their bellies decides for them.


Micro-tension to weave in


- Status signal: Cold basins are for everyone; hot rooms imply money, membership, or reputation. Make both of them notice the social line being crossed.
- Consent and boundaries: Let Pontodoros offer the choice twice—once at the cold basins (“If you prefer the cold, no offense taken” - with a glance at Rimmon) and once for the dinner invitation (“If you don’t come, there'll be no debt between us.”). Andrej has to mention Rimmon at this point, and Pontodoros keeps his face motionless, just extends the invitation to him as well. Maybe he asks one sharp question. "What is he to you?", this could be a juicy question for Andrej to answer. He does not know yet if the city is really as lenient and progressive as everybody says it is.


Setting details that sell it


- A short walkway from sanded wrestling ground to water: oil, dust, sweat, and scraped skin on stone—sensory contrast.
- The hot section: steam escaping a curtain/door, a guard/attendant who recognizes Pontodoros, quieter acoustics, fewer eyes. Beautiful wall murals of bearded men fighting, bathing, eating, wine, dance, all of it. A lot of lush greenery. It should feel massively luxurious. Andrej is stunned by the water alone - he comes from a frickin' desert village. Maybe he can also notice another "male couple" in the baths who feed each other grapes, jaw dropping for a second.
- “The Prosperous Voyage” signage: carved prow or painted ship’s eye; smells of fish-sauce, wine, resin, and tar from the harbor; serves different seafood dishes: Grilled or roasted whole fish, Tuna or mackerel steaks, Octopus, Mussels folded into a grain dish, Anchovy pilaf. Plus different fruit, vegetables, watered down wine as a drink. Something neither Andrej nor Rimmon have ever eaten.

## Grok

The arena master called Andrej’s name again, voice booming over the rising murmur of the crowd. Two more bouts. Two more chances. The purse from the first win already weighed heavy at his belt—copper coins clinking with every step—but it wasn’t enough. Not yet.


The second opponent was a wiry youth named Lysander, barely older than Andrej himself, quick as a lizard, all elbows and knees. Lysander danced around him, feinting left, right, trying to hook a leg or slip behind. Andrej planted his feet in the center of the sand, oil-slick skin gleaming under the sun, and waited.
Lysander tired faster than Claudius had. When the opening came—a fraction too long between lunges—Andrej stepped in, caught him around the waist in a bear hug that lifted him clean off the ground, and simply walked him backward until Lysander’s heels crossed the line.
The crowd roared louder this time. Coins rained into the sand. Someone shouted “Silver-Eyes! Silver-Eyes!” like it was already a name.


The third opponent was bigger—almost Andrej’s height, broader across the shoulders, a man named Drakon who moved like he’d been born fighting. Drakon tried power first: bull-rushes, shoulder checks, trying to drive Andrej straight out. Andrej absorbed each impact, feet sliding only inches before he reset, refusing to give ground. When Drakon finally overcommitted—lunging too far forward—Andrej sidestepped, hooked an arm around Drakon’s neck, and used the man’s own momentum to spin him out of the ring in a controlled, almost elegant throw.


Three wins.
Three purses.
Enough for a washhouse, dinner, and—maybe—a room for the night.


The arena master raised Andrej’s arm, grinning wide. “One more, ox! The champion’s purse is double. Pontodoros waits.”


The crowd hushed, then swelled again with anticipation.


Pontodoros stepped into the ring.


He was older—late thirties, maybe forty—broad as a bull, thick through the chest and arms, silver threading his dark beard. He moved slowly, deliberately, the way mountains move: inevitable. No flourish, no posturing. He simply stood in the center of the sand, arms loose at his sides, and waited.


Andrej felt the shift in the air. The cheers quieted. Even the vendors fell silent. Pontodoros had fought here for years, the arena master had whispered earlier—undefeated in three seasons, only retired because he chose to, not because anyone had forced him. He had come back today “for amusement,” the master said.
Amusement.


They circled once.
Pontodoros moved like water around stone—fluid, patient, never wasting energy. Andrej planted again, feet wide, refusing to chase. Pontodoros didn’t rush. He waited for Andrej to overcommit, then closed the distance in a single, rolling step.


The first grip came fast—Pontodoros’ arms wrapping around Andrej’s waist from behind, lifting him an inch off the sand before Andrej could twist free. The crowd gasped. Andrej landed hard, sand spraying, but he stayed upright.
Pontodoros smiled—small, almost kind—and reset.


They grappled again. Andrej tried the same immovable stance that had worked three times. Pontodoros simply walked into him, shoulder to shoulder, driving with steady, relentless pressure. Andrej pushed back, muscles straining, oil making every hold slippery and uncertain. For a moment he thought he had it—Pontodoros’ foot slipped half an inch—and Andrej surged forward, trying to hook a leg and throw.


Pontodoros anticipated it.
He pivoted, used Andrej’s momentum against him, and suddenly Andrej was airborne—lifted, twisted, and dropped hard onto the sand with a thud that knocked the breath from his lungs.


The crowd roared.


Andrej rolled, gasping, tasting blood where he’d bitten his tongue. He got to his knees. Pontodoros stood over him, not gloating, just waiting.


Andrej lunged one last time—desperate, reckless—trying to take the older man down by sheer force. Pontodoros caught him mid-motion, arms locking around Andrej’s ribs in a crushing grip, then simply walked him backward—step by deliberate step—until Andrej’s heels crossed the line and he stumbled out of the ring.


The crowd exploded.


Pontodoros raised both hands—not in victory, but in acknowledgment. He looked down at Andrej, sprawled in the dust just outside the stones, and offered a hand up.


Andrej took it.


Pontodoros pulled him to his feet with surprising gentleness, clapped him once on the shoulder, and murmured in a voice low enough for only Andrej to hear:


“Good fight, boy. You’ve got heart. And strength. Come find me in the baths if you want to learn how to use both.”


He walked away without another word.


An attendant was already there—older, scarred, brusque—checking Andrej’s head, rinsing blood from his split lip with a damp cloth, wrapping a shallow cut on his forearm. “No concussion,” the man grunted. “You’ll bruise, but nothing broken. Go wash. Cold basins are that way.”


Andrej nodded, still breathing hard, sand clinging to his oiled skin. He looked for Rimmon.


Rimmon was already pushing through the crowd, face pale, eyes wide with worry and something fiercer. He reached Andrej in seconds, hands hovering like he wanted to touch but didn’t know where it was safe.


“You’re bleeding,” Rimmon said, voice tight.


“It’s nothing.” Andrej tried to smile. It hurt. “I won three. Lost the last. We’ve got coin.”


Rimmon’s fingers finally settled on Andrej’s jaw, gentle, turning his face to check the split lip. “You’re an idiot,” he whispered, but his thumb brushed away a smear of blood with a tenderness that made Andrej’s chest ache.


The attendant jerked his head toward the walkway leading to the wash area. “Cold basins are for everyone. Hot rooms are for winners and guests. Your choice.”


Andrej glanced at Rimmon. Rimmon’s mouth thinned—he knew what cold water on fresh bruises felt like—but he nodded once. “Cold is fine.”


They started toward the outdoor basins—stone troughs fed by a mountain spring, water icy even in summer—when a voice called from behind.


“Wait.”


Pontodoros stood at the entrance to a curtained doorway, steam curling out around him like smoke. He was still oiled, still bare except for the loincloth, but he looked different here—calmer, more at home.


“The cold water is for the poor,” he said, voice carrying easily over the noise. “Today you’ve earned more. Come—enjoy the hot rooms.”


He was speaking only to Andrej.


Andrej froze. Rimmon’s hand tightened on his arm, just slightly.


Pontodoros glanced at Rimmon—once, assessingly—then back to Andrej. “Bring your friend if you like. Or come alone. No debt either way.”


He disappeared behind the curtain without waiting for an answer.


Andrej turned to Rimmon. Rimmon’s face was carefully blank, but his eyes were stormy.


“He’s offering hot baths,” Andrej said quietly. “And… dinner. At his taverna. The Prosperous Voyage. Sunset. Harbor district. He said if we don’t come, there’s no debt.”


Rimmon’s jaw worked. “We don’t need some rich patron’s coin,” he said, voice low and hard. “Aren’t you sick of being owned by someone?”


Andrej exhaled. “It’s a free meal. We’re hungry. And hot water would help your back.”


Rimmon looked away, toward the cold basins, then back to the curtained doorway where steam still drifted. He was silent for a long moment.


Then he nodded once, sharp. “Fine. Hot water. Dinner. But if he tries to buy you, we walk.”


Andrej took Rimmon’s hand—firm, unhesitating. “We walk.”


They stepped through the curtain together.


The hot rooms were another world: steam rising in thick, scented clouds, walls painted with faded frescoes of bearded men wrestling, bathing, feasting, dancing, drinking wine. Lush greenery spilled from pots—ferns, vines, jasmine—softening the stone. A few other men lounged on benches or soaked in the long, rectangular pools, steam curling around them like veils. One pair—two young men, clearly lovers—fed each other grapes from a bowl, laughing softly. Andrej stared for half a heartbeat, stunned by the casual openness of it.


Pontodoros waited at the edge of the largest pool, already submerged to the chest, steam rising around his broad shoulders. He lifted a hand in greeting—calm, welcoming, no triumph in it.


“Sit,” he said simply. “Soak. The cold will only make the bruises worse.”


Andrej hesitated. Rimmon’s grip on his hand tightened, then relaxed.


They stepped down into the water together.


The heat was shocking after the cold basins—almost painful at first, then soothing, sinking into every aching muscle, every scraped knuckle, every bruise. Andrej let out a breath he hadn’t realized he was holding.


Pontodoros watched them settle, expression unreadable. Then he spoke, voice low, carrying over the soft lap of water.


“Eat with me at sunset,” he said. “The Prosperous Voyage. Harbor district. Painted ship’s eye over the door. You’ll smell the fish sauce and wine before you see it.” He glanced at Rimmon—once, openly—then back to Andrej. “Both of you. If you don’t come, there’ll be no debt between us.”


He paused.


“What is he to you?” The question was quiet, almost gentle, but it landed like a thrown knife.


Andrej looked at Rimmon—hair damp, cheeks flushed from the steam, eyes fierce and protective—and felt the answer rise without hesitation.


“He’s mine,” he said.


Rimmon’s hand found his under the water, fingers threading together.


Pontodoros nodded once—small, satisfied—and rose from the pool, water streaming from his broad frame.


“Sunset, then,” he said, and walked away.


Andrej turned to Rimmon. Rimmon’s eyes were bright, mouth curved in that small, vicious, hopeful smile.


“Yours, am I?” he murmured.


Andrej squeezed his hand. “Always have been.”


They soaked in silence after that, letting the heat take the ache from their bones, letting the city’s hum fade to a distant song.


Sunset was hours away.
The Prosperous Voyage waited.


And for the first time since they’d run, Andrej wasn’t afraid of what came next.


They were together.
They were honest.
And they were hungry. 😈

Andrej’s name was announced again, the voice of the arena master booming over the rising murmur of the crowd. Two more bouts. Two more chances. The purse from the first win already weighed heavy at his belt—copper coins clinking with every step—but it wasn’t enough. Not yet.
The second opponent was a wiry youth named Lysander, barely older than Andrej himself, quick as a lizard, all elbows and knees. The older man’s stance was deceptive—feet shoulder-width, weight balanced, arms loose, as if he had all the time in the world. Andrej feinted left, testing. Pontodoros didn’t bite. Just watched, dark eyes unreadable, tracking Andrej like a predator sizing up prey.
Andrej lunged first, fingers aiming for Pontodoros’s wrist. Their skin met—oil-slick, hot—and for a heartbeat, Andrej’s pulse stuttered. Pontodoros’s forearm was corded with muscle, the hair rough against his palm. Then Pontodoros sidestepped, fluid as water, and Andrej’s momentum carried him forward—too far. A hand clamped around his waist, fingers digging into the dip of his spine, lifting him an inch off the sand. The crowd gasped. Andrej twisted, but Pontodoros’s grip was iron, his body a wall of heat and sweat.

Andrej refused to chase. He planted his feet, knees bent, and waited. Pontodoros came at him like a storm, shoulder first, driving him back. Their bodies collided—chest to chest, oil and sweat sealing them together. Andrej gritted his teeth, the scent of Pontodoros filling his nose: salt, leather, and something darker, muskier. He pushed back, muscles straining, but Pontodoros was relentless, his breath hot against Andrej’s ear.
“You’re strong,” Pontodoros growled, “but you waste it.”
His hand slid up Andrej’s back, fingers pressing into the tension there, almost a caress. Andrej’s skin burned where he touched. He surged forward, trying to hook a leg, but Pontodoros anticipated it. With a twist of his hips, he sent Andrej flying. The sand rushed up to meet him, the impact knocking the breath from his lungs.
Andrej rolled, gasping. Pontodoros stood over him, legs braced, watching him with something like approval—or hunger. The crowd’s roar was a distant thing. All Andrej could hear was his own heartbeat, the rasp of Pontodoros’s breathing.
Andrej lunged again—desperate, reckless. Pontodoros caught him mid-motion, arms locking around his ribs in a crushing grip. Andrej’s vision blurred, the arena tilting. He was lifted, twisted—airborne—then dropped. The impact sent a crack through his bones. Sand filled his mouth, gritty and bitter.
The crowd’s roar was a thunderclap. Pontodoros’s hands were under his arms now, hauling him up. Andrej’s heels crossed the line, and the transition from soft sand to the sun-baked stone of the perimeter bit into his soles like a jagged blade.

Their faces were inches apart, Pontodoros’s dark eyes searching his. “You’ve got heart. And strength,” he said, voice low. His thumb brushed Andrej’s wrist, just once. “Come find me in the baths if you want to learn how to use both.”
The crowd’s cheers were a storm, but Andrej barely heard them. His ribs ached, his tongue throbbed where he’d bitten it. The taste of copper filled his mouth. Pontodoros turned away, but not before Andrej caught the flicker of a smile, the promise in his eyes.
Andrej stood, legs unsteady, the weight of the purses suddenly meaningless. The heat of Pontodoros’s hand still lingered on his skin.
An attendant grunted beside him, poking at a cut on his shoulder. The cloth was cool but the water was stinking of vinegar. “No concussion. You’ll bruise, but nothing’s broken. Go wash. The basins are that way.”
Andrej nodded, his chest heaving. Every breath felt like drawing air through a wet wool blanket. He looked for Rimmon.
Rimmon was already there, his face pale against the vibrant colors of the crowd. When he reached Andrej, he didn’t touch him at first; he hovered, his hands twitching as if he were trying to map the damage without making it worse.
“You’re bleeding,” Rimmon said, his voice tight with a familiar, protective anger.
“It’s nothing.” Andrej wanted to laugh. Of course I’m bleeding. But all he managed was a shaky smile. The skin of his cheek felt tight and hot, already beginning to swell. “I won three. Lost the last. But look, we’ve got coin now.” He pressed the heavy leather pouch into Rimmon’s hands.
Rimmon ignored the money. His fingers settled on Andrej’s jaw, surprisingly warm, turning his face toward the light. “You’re an idiot,” he whispered. The thumb that brushed his bruised cheek was so gentle it made the ache in Andrej’s ribs feel twice as heavy.
The walk to the basins was a blur of heavy limbs and the smell of drying blood. The water in the cold tanks was brutal. When Andrej ducked his head under, the shock silenced the ringing in his ears, replacing it with a sharp, crystalline clarity. He scrubbed at the oil and sand, watching pink-tinged water swirl into the drains.
Rimmon hovered nearby, arms crossed, his usual warmth replaced by a tight, worried silence. Andrej caught his eye, tried to smile, but Rimmon only scowled. “You let him throw you,” he muttered. “You could’ve won.”
Andrej opened his mouth to argue, but a shadow fell over them.
Pontodoros stood there, a towel slung over one shoulder, his oiled skin gleaming. “The cold water is for the poor,” he said, “or those who prefer punishment to pleasure. Today, you’ve earned more.” He looked at Rimmon, a slow, assessing sweep of his eyes, then back to Andrej. “Come, Ironborn. Enjoy the hot steam.”
Andrej’s breath hitched at the word. It was tugging at something deep inside him, something that had been buried under sand for a long time.
Rimmon’s jaw tightened. Andrej hesitated, aware of the social line only he was being asked to cross. The cold basins were for everyone; the hot rooms were for patrons, veterans, men of status. Pontodoros was offering him a place among them.
“I—” Andrej glanced at Rimmon, who looked away, lips pressed thin.
Pontodoros’s expression didn’t change. “If you prefer the cold, no offense taken.” He turned, as if to leave, then paused. “But if you want to learn how to fight more like other Ironborn…” He let the words hang, then disappeared behind a steam-wreathed curtain. He knows, Andrej thought. He knows I don’t know what an Ironborn is, and he noticed it makes my blood boil.
“We don’t need his charity,” Rimmon snapped.
Andrej looked at the steam-wreathed curtain where Pontodoros had disappeared. He could feel the cold water leaching the last of his strength. “It’s not charity. It’s an invitation.”
“To what?” Rimmon’s voice was sharp.
“To an answer,” Andrej said quietly. Forgive me, he thought, and stepped toward the heat.
Inside, the air was a thick, humid embrace that smelled of eucalyptus and wood-smoke. It was a world of soft edges. The floor was smooth mosaic, slick under his bare feet. He saw the guard in the eagle-embossed chestplate, then the two men in the corner pool, lounging in a way that made Andrej’s heart skip. They were open. Unafraid.
Pontodoros gestured to a sunken bath where the water rolled in gentle ripples. “Sit. Then we’ll talk.”
He didn’t wait for an answer, stripping off his loincloth with effortless confidence and stepping into the water. Andrej followed suit, though with less grace, the heat seeping into his bones the moment he sank beneath the surface.
Guilt caught up to him, a bitter tang on his tongue. Rimmon wasn’t here. Andrej would have to come up with a fitting atonement. But first, this.
Pontodoros leaned back against the bath’s edge, watching Andrej through half-lidded eyes. “You fight like a man who’s used to losing,” he said. “But you don’t have to.”
Andrej wiped a hand over his face, clearing the sweat. “I usually don’t fight, so it is rare for me the lose.”
“Is that so?” Pontodoros’s smile was knowing. “Why did you let me throw you?”
Andrej’s chest tightened. He glanced toward the curtains, the courtyard beyond, where the cold basins sat like a rebuke. “I didn’t let you.”
Pontodoros chuckled. “You are a horrible liar. So…” He reached for a clay jug of watered wine, pouring two cups, handing one to Andrej. “You seemed quite confused when I addressed you as Ironborn. How come?”
Andrej looked down at his arms. In the golden light of the steam room, the fine blond hairs shimmered. He saw the contrast of his pale shoulders against the darker tan of his forearms—the mark of a life spent in the sun, but born of a different soil. “I was raised in the desert,” he admitted.
Pontodoros moved closer, his hand landing on Andrej’s shoulder. The heat of the man’s palm matched the water. “The desert? With the Luedina? Oh, you poor thing. No wonder they let a gem like you go to waste.” He took a sip of wine, his eyes fixed on Andrej’s. “Eat with me at sunset. I own a taverna: The Prosperous Voyage. If you don’t come, there’ll be no debt between us.”
Andrej’s fingers tightened around his cup, he broke the intimate moment, now looking back toward the courtyard, where Rimmon—furious, hurt—was waiting outside. He felt the weight of Pontodoros’s hand on his shoulder, the promise in his voice.
Pontodoros followed his gaze. “The other one,” he said. “What is he to you?”
Andrej’s throat closed. The words my home. My everything lodged in his throat. He cleared it, forcing himself to meet Pontodoros’s eyes again. “My partner,” he said, the word heavy, final. “Meaning I’d like you to extend your invitation to us both.”
A moment of silence stretched, thick with the smell of eucalyptus. Pontodoros studied him, then gave a slow, respectful nod. He settled back into a more conversational, easy grace. “I see. Well, then I am happy to have him as well.”
Rimmon was drying himself with sharp, angry motions by the cold basins when Andrej found him. His back was to Andrej, shoulders rigid.
“Pontodoros invited us to dinner,” Andrej said.


## Can you draft Rimmon's POV? Ooooh our poor cute Rimmon, hah!

## Grok

Rimmon stood at the edge of the cold basins, scrubbing the last of the sand and oil from his arms with furious, mechanical motions. The water was ice—sharp enough to numb the ache in his back, sharp enough to make him hiss through his teeth—but he welcomed it. Anything to drown out the heat still crawling under his skin from watching Andrej in the ring: oiled, gleaming, immovable, every muscle a reminder of what those same muscles had done to him last night.


He had told himself he was fine with it.
He had told himself he could watch Andrej fight, watch him win, watch him *lose* without letting it twist into something jealous or possessive.
He had lied.


The curtain to the hot rooms had parted like a wound, and Pontodoros had stepped through—broad, silvered, calm as a mountain—and offered Andrej something Rimmon couldn’t give: heat, luxury, the quiet promise of a place among men who understood power without apology. And Andrej had hesitated—just for a heartbeat—but he had *looked* at the steam, at the promise, and then he had looked at Rimmon with those silver eyes that always saw too much, and Rimmon had felt the ground shift beneath him.


*He’s going,* Rimmon thought, the words bitter as bile. *He’s going in there and I’m staying out here with the cold water and the poor.*


He knew it wasn’t fair. He knew Andrej hadn’t chosen to leave him behind. He knew the invitation had been for Andrej alone, and that Andrej had asked for him to be included. He knew all of it.
And still, the anger burned.


He wrung out the cloth with more force than necessary, water dripping between his fingers like accusation. His back throbbed—old scars pulling tight from the day’s walking, from last night’s abandon—and he hated how much he wanted to lean into Andrej’s careful hands again. Hated how much he wanted to be the one Andrej carried, not just in the ring but always. Hated how small he felt standing here, cold and damp and left outside.


Footsteps on stone. Rimmon didn’t turn.


Andrej’s voice came low, careful, the way it always did when he thought Rimmon might shatter. “Pontodoros invited us to dinner. The Prosperous Voyage. Sunset. Harbor district.”


Rimmon’s shoulders went rigid. He kept scrubbing, harder now, as if he could scour the jealousy out of his own skin.


“Us,” he repeated, flat.


“He asked what you are to me.” Andrej stepped closer—close enough that Rimmon could feel the heat radiating off him, the faint scent of eucalyptus and oil clinging to his skin. “I told him you’re my partner.”


Rimmon finally turned. Andrej stood there, still damp from the steam, hair sticking to his forehead, silver eyes earnest and worried and so damn *gentle* it made Rimmon want to scream.


“Your partner,” Rimmon echoed, and the word tasted like ash. “And he just… accepted that? Extended the invitation like it was nothing?”


Andrej nodded once. “He did.”


Rimmon laughed—short, sharp, bitter. “Of course he did. This is Mergos. They’re so fucking *liberal* they probably have statues of men fucking in the forum. Why wouldn’t he invite your little desert stray along for the meal?”


Andrej flinched—just a flicker—but Rimmon saw it. Saw the guilt. Saw the way Andrej’s hand twitched like he wanted to reach out but didn’t know if he was allowed.


“I didn’t ask for the invitation,” Andrej said quietly. “I asked for you to be included. Because I don’t go anywhere without you.”


Rimmon’s throat closed. He looked away, toward the cold basins, the water still dripping from the spout in slow, accusing drops.


“I don’t want his charity,” he said, voice cracking despite himself. “I don’t want to sit at his table and watch him look at you like you’re something he could buy. Or keep. Or—” He stopped, swallowing hard. “I don’t want to be the one he tolerates because I’m attached to you.”


Andrej stepped forward then—slow, careful, the way he always approached when Rimmon was fraying at the edges. His hand found Rimmon’s wrist, thumb pressing gently against the pulse there.


“You’re not attached to me,” Andrej said, voice rough with something fierce and tender. “You’re not an afterthought. You’re not charity. You’re mine. And I’m yours. And if Pontodoros wants to feed us, let him. We’re hungry. We’re tired. We’re bruised. But we’re not his. We never will be.”


Rimmon looked up at him—at the split lip, the bruise blooming across his cheekbone, the silver eyes that had carried him through a desert and still looked at him like he was worth everything.


He felt the anger crack, just a little.


“You’re an idiot,” he whispered, but there was no heat in it anymore. Just exhaustion. Just relief.


“I know.” Andrej’s thumb stroked once across the inside of Rimmon’s wrist. “But I’m your idiot.”


Rimmon exhaled—a shaky, half-laugh—and leaned forward until his forehead rested against Andrej’s collarbone. The skin there was still warm from the steam, still slick with oil and sweat and the faint scent of myrrh.


“Sunset,” Rimmon said at last. “We go. We eat. We leave if it feels wrong. But if he looks at you like you’re for sale—”


Andrej’s arms came around him—careful, solid, grounding.


“Then we walk,” he finished. “Together.”


Rimmon closed his eyes. The cold water still dripped behind them. The city hummed beyond the wall. The scent of roasted fish and cinnamon drifted on the breeze.


And for the first time since the tent, since the oil and the lamp and the breaking, Rimmon let himself believe—really believe—that they might actually make it.


That they might be terrible together, and still be enough.


He lifted his head, met Andrej’s gaze, and smiled—small, vicious, hopeful.


“Sunset, then,” he said. “Let’s go see if we can eat without selling our souls.”


Andrej squeezed his hand once—firm, certain, a vow.


“Together,” he promised.


And they walked—side by side, bruised and hungry and alive—toward whatever came next. 😈

How would you describe Mergos in four sentences?

---

## Grok

Mergos rises where three great trade roads converge and the Wide Sea is bridged by the ancient Aetius Span, a city-state that has always paid tribute to everyone and bowed to no one.
Its architecture marries the austere geometry of Nova Prima with lingering Elven grace—pale stone towers wrapped in silver filigree, arches that curve like living vines, rooftops spilling over with impossible gardens of jasmine and pomegranate.
The streets hum with the constant pulse of merchants, mercenaries, spies, and exiles; every corner smells of cinnamon, roasted fish, ink from the bookbinders, and the faint metallic tang of forges that never sleep.
It is a place where names are currency, secrets are traded like grain, and two dusty boys with scars and no past can vanish into the crowd—or be seen exactly for what they are—and still walk away hand in hand.

They found a shop tucked between a spice merchant and a jeweler. The sign was a simple bolt of violet silk hanging over the door. Inside, the air was cool and smelled of pressed lavender.
