Internally, Andrej’s blood simmered. He hated the way she categorized them, relegated him to the shadow of the 'god.' But he kept his face a mask of granite, his posture loose and deceptively bored, even as his pulse thrummed against his collar.
Rimmon drew himself up with a dramatic inhalation, his vanity—always his most reliable defense—sparking to life. He made sure to loom impressively over the woman. “We need something for dinner with a man of status,” he declared, his voice regaining its melodic, sing-song lilt.
“A man of status,” she repeated, her gaze traveling down the line of Rimmon’s throat. “Well, you have come to the right hands, let me see.”
She assessed Andrej first, her professional eye measuring the width of his shoulders. With a faint tch, she dismissed the finer linens on display and vanished into the back, returning with a tunic of thick, creamy wool that smelled of lanolin and a bundle of thick, brown cloth. “For the Guardian something practical. And for this one...”
She reached out, her fingers inches from Rimmon’s hair. “Is it true what the sailors say? That the desert-born are lions in the dark?” She flashed a sharp, white smile. “I’ve always wanted to see a desert lion up close. See if the mane is as soft as it looks.”
Rimmon saw the minute tightening of Andrej’s jaw—the tiny, delicious fracture in his “cool” exterior. He leaned into the woman’s space, almost brushing her hennaed fingers with his temple. He didn't look at her; he looked at Andrej, his eyes dancing with a wicked, taunting light.
“It’s softer,” Rimmon whispered, his gaze locked on his friend. “But the claws are real. Aren't they, Andrej?”
Andrej felt the hook sink in and twist. He wanted to haul Rimmon back by the scruff of his neck, away from her scent and her appraisal. Instead, he simply shed his old tunic and put on the new garment, his expression as cold as a mountain stream, while his mind screamed at the reckless, preening fool beside him.
The woman laughed, a sound like silver coins hitting a tray. For Rimmon, she pulled a smaller tunic from the shelf. “The same foundation. But for the God...” Her fingers trailed along a shelf before landing on a folded bolt of fabric the color of fresh blood. She shook it out with a snap, and the room seemed to brighten. It was fine-combed wool, lighter than Andrej's, meant for show, not snow. “The himation,” she said, the word a purr. “In white, you are a citizen. In this?” She let the lush fabric pool at Rimmon's feet. “You are a poem. And every poem... requires a certain deliberate carelessness in its composition.” She gestured to a marble bench behind a freestanding screen painted with herons. “Your travel dust will offend the linen.” She Pointed at Rimmon. “Come with me.”
The new tunic sat snug across Andrej’s chest, but he enjoyed wearing something fresh and clean for once. He was trying to remember the last time he bought himself clothing, but his thoughts got interrupted by the soft rustle of Rimmon's himation behind the screen, followed by the woman's low, appreciative hum. It wasn't a sound for him. He stared at the wall, listening to the intimate silence of the new cloth being lifted, the whisper as it settled over another man's skin, the barely-there gasp of a pin finding its hold.
Andrej let out a grunt and pulled the umber Paenula—a hooded, bell-shaped cloak—over his shoulders, fastening the singular button with trembling hands. I guess this is what I deserve for the hot baths, he thought to himself.
When they both emerged from behind the screen, Rimmon looked... respectable. The crimson wool was arranged around his hips, over his left shoulder, and finally flowed over his forearm in the classic, stately curve. With a provocative glance towards Andrej, she hooked a single finger into the fabric near Rimmon's chest and gave it the slightest, most deliberate tug. The elegant line broke; a fold loosened over his shoulder, exposing a bit more of the white tunic beneath, creating a glimpse of skin and a sense of dynamic, tempting disorder. “There,” she whispered, stepping back. “Now it is alive. It has a story. It says, I do not need to try... but you may try for me.”
Rimmon examined the crimson drape over his forearm, then lifted his gaze to Andrej. A slow, cat-like smile spread across his face. ”Hm. It seems some garments are made for standing guard,” he said, his voice a honeyed murmur. “And others are made for being... unwrapped.”
He’s enjoying this, Andrej realized, the jealousy curdling into something hotter, sharper. He wants to see if I’ll snap. Andrej forced a slow, even breath through his nose and crossed his arms. The scent of lavender was suddenly cloying. He gave Rimmon a single, flat look that said, I see your game. And you will lose. The muscle in his jaw, however, had gone rock-hard.
“Good,” she said, clapping her hands once, deciding the flirtation had served its purpose. ”Because dinner with a man of status is not about cloth. It’s about stories. And your feet are telling the wrong one.”
She quickly assessed their feet, then swept to a cabinet and produced two pairs of sandals. One pair was quickly shoved into Andrej’s hands, but when she handed the second pair to Rimmon, her fingers trailed over his palm, a deliberate, lingering contact.
They took the sandals, the new leather fine and supple compared to the cracked, salt-bitten soles of their old ones. Andrej sat on a low stool, his movements jerky as he laced the thin straps up his calves, his focus entirely on the floor to avoid looking at the vendor’s hands on Rimmon. Beside him, Rimmon didn't sit; he balanced with the easy grace of a dancer, sliding his feet into the cradles of leather and tightening the buckles with a flourish. They stood together—the transition from dusty outcasts to men of the city complete from the ground up.
“City-sandals,” the woman said. “And remember, a Himation is an art form. It requires... constant attention to keep the drape just so.” She leaned in, her voice dropping to a sultry murmur intended for only Rimmon to hear. “If you require any assistance in adjusting your Himation in the future, Lion... you know exactly where to find me.”
Andrej let out a breath that could have been a laugh. He let his hands fall to his sides. They were open, loose. A fighter’s calm. Inside, he was mapping the fastest way to put himself between Rimmon and this woman, between Rimmon and the door, between Rimmon and his own damned, provocative smile.
Then, louder, the vendor tapped Rimmon’s new sandals with one fingernail. ”And you—before you dine—you will visit the barber three doors down.”
Rimmon’s brows arched. He tossed his hair back, a full, theatrical gesture. ”My hair is perfect.”
“A lion is a noble beast,” she said sweetly, not looking at Rimmon, but at Andrej, as if they were two parents discussing an unruly child, ”but a wild mane belongs in the waste, not at a table of politics.” She now addressed Andrej directly: ”Please take him.”
“And if I don’t?” Andrej asked, his own voice dangerously low.
Her smile sharpened. She finally looked at Rimmon, her appraisal open, shameless. ”Then I’ll keep him.” She gestured around the shop at the headless mannequins draped in finery. ”I have displays. I have very strong pins. I have all the time in the world to admire a living sculpture.”
Rimmon’s grin turned truly wicked. He played with the crimson tail over his shoulder with a flamboyant flourish, looking at Andrej with a silent dare.
“Careful,” Rimmon told the woman, his voice a playful chime. “Sculptures stand still. I am very well able to move.”
“Then move to the barber,” she laughed, shooing them toward the light of the street. “Before I decide to pin you to the wall myself. But first”
They stood together, the transformation nearly complete, but the discarded remnants of their journey remained on the floor. Andrej reached down and gathered his leather belt and buckled it over the new tunica.
Rimmon watched him, then reached into the heap of their old travel-dusted clothing. He plucked out his own worn pouch and the old brown headscarf—the one Andrej had made for him. With a look that was uncharacteristically solemn, he stepped close to Andrej and tucked both items into the travel pack over Andrej’s shoulder. He patted the leather, a silent surrender of his burdens, entrusting his few memories to Andrej’s keeping. Their eyes met for a brief, quiet second, something softer passing between them—a silent vow that no matter the finery, they were still the men who had bled on the road together.
Andrej’s hand closed around Rimmon’s upper arm—not roughly, but with an undeniable, guiding force. It was the grip of a guardian, a handler, a man at the very end of his patience. He steered the living sculpture, the poem in red wool, out into the sun-drenched chaos of the market, the vendor’s silver-coin laughter and the soft clink of freshly counted payment chasing them out the door.
The barber shop was a shrine to masculinity, and Rimmon felt like a sacrilege walking through the door. The walls were lined with polished bronze mirrors that caught the light and threw it back like accusations. The air smelled of sharp citrus oil and the metallic tang of shears. Three chairs, all occupied—an elderly merchant getting his beard trimmed, a young soldier with a fresh scar across his cheek, and a boy no older than twelve who flinched every time the scissors snipped near his ears.
I don’t belong here, Rimmon thought, but Andrej’s hand was still on his arm, guiding him forward with a pressure that said you’re not leaving.
The barber was a Luedina man, dark and wiry, with hands that moved like spiders—quick, precise, and utterly without mercy. He looked up as they entered, his gaze sweeping over Rimmon’s hair with the clinical assessment of a man about to amputate a limb.
“By the gods,” the barber muttered, setting down his shears. “Did you drag that through a sandstorm or a battle?”
“Both,” Andrej said before Rimmon could answer, his voice flat. “The woman at the silk shop sent him.”
“The violet silk,” the barber said, nodding. “She has a way of finding strays.” He gestured to the empty chair with a flick of his wrist. “Sit. Let’s see what can be salvaged.”
Rimmon sat. The leather was cool against his bare back, still tender from the dye pits. The mirror showed him a stranger—hair a wild tangle, eyes too bright, mouth set in a defiant line that looked more like a plea. He watched Andrej move to the side of the shop, lean against the wall, cross his arms. His expression was stone, but his knuckles were white.
“How short?” the barber asked, already combing his fingers through the mess, working out knots with a ruthlessness that made Rimmon’s eyes water.
“Short enough to look respectable,” Andrej said. “Not so short that he looks like a soldier.”
The barber’s hands paused. He glanced at Andrej in the mirror, then at Rimmon, and a small, knowing smile touched his lips. “Ah. I see.”
You see nothing, Rimmon wanted to snap. You see a boy playing at being a man, and a man playing at being a guardian, and you think you understand. But he held his tongue. He was too tired to perform.
The scissors started. The sound was violent—snick, snick, snick—and with each cut, he felt something drop away. Not just hair, but the last vestige of the boy who had walked out of Meddūr’s house with a pack on his back and a head full of poetry. The curls fell to the floor in dark, broken clusters. The barber worked quickly, shaping the sides, leaving the top long enough to comb back, to soften the angles of his face.
Rimmon watched himself disappear strand by strand. He thought of the carved hand in the pouch at his hip, the crack in its lifeline. He thought of Andrej’s hand on his arm, the way it had tightened when the shopkeeper had leaned too close. He thought of Pontodoros, offering a place at a table where he didn’t have to be a spectacle.
The barber stepped back, shears held aloft like a conductor’s baton. “There. Civilized.”
Rimmon looked in the mirror. The man staring back was… unfamiliar. Sharper. Older. The wildness was gone, replaced by something that looked like control. He looked like a man who could sit at a table of politics and not flinch.
He looked like someone who belonged in Mergos.
And he hated it.
Andrej moved behind him, his reflection appearing in the mirror over Rimmon’s shoulder. Their eyes met—silver meeting dark—and Andrej’s expression finally cracked. Not anger. Not possession. Something softer. Something like grief.
“You look like him,” Andrej said quietly. “The heir.”
Rimmon’s heart skipped. “I look like a man,” he corrected, voice thin. “That’s the point.”
Andrej’s hand settled on his shoulder, heavy and warm. “You were already a man. Now you just look like you think you have to prove it.”
The barber coughed, suddenly very interested in cleaning his shears. The other patrons were watching now, the story unfolding in the mirrors like a play they’d paid to see.
Rimmon stood, the new tunic falling into place, the himation draping over his arm just as the shopkeeper had shown him. He looked at himself one last time—civilized, respectable, a poem with its edges sanded smooth.
Then he reached up, grabbed a handful of the carefully arranged hair, and mussed it until it stood up in wild, defiant spikes.
“There,” he said to Andrej, and his smile was small and vicious and real. “Now it’s alive.”
He tossed a copper coin to the barber—more than the cut was worth—and walked out of the shop, the himation draped crookedly over his shoulder, the poem deliberately misspelled.
Andrej followed, one step behind, the guardian who had finally learned that his charge didn’t need protecting from the world.
He just needed protecting from becoming it.


Guilt lodged heavy behind Andrej’s ribs, thick and cold as wet sand. The memory of the baths—of steam curling around Pontodoros’ shoulders, of the murmured invitation that felt like a hook in his gut—caught up with him the moment they stepped back into the sun. Rimmon had said he was still angry, now on top of that he looked said. The thought made his stomach twist.


He hurried to close the distance. His hand found Rimmon’s without asking, fingers sliding between warm ones and closing tight. Rimmon didn’t resist, but he didn’t squeeze back either.


Andrej tugged gently, steering them out of the main flow of the market into a quieter side street. A shallow archway offered shadow and a moment of stillness; the stone was cool against their backs as they stepped inside. Their chests were almost touching in the narrow space. Andrej could smell the sharp citrus oil from the barber on Rimmon’s skin, overlaid with the faint trace of mint.


“You look sad,” he said quietly.


Rimmon’s gaze flicked up, then away again—toward the bright street, toward the people passing by without a glance.


## “I am sorry for letting them cut your hair,” Andrej went on. His free hand came up, kneading the knuckles of the other as if he could wring the words into the right shape. “I am sorry about the baths. I just… he mentioned Ironborn, and I felt this pull. Like he might help me solve a riddle about myself.” His voice dropped lower, almost ashamed. He reached up, cupped Rimmon’s jaw with one hand, his thumb brushing the sharp line of his cheekbone. “All this time, we just did what someone else wanted. But the only thing I want is for you to be happy.”

Rimmon is devastated.. look at this internal monologue: **Inside Rimmon, everything was collapsing into a single, suffocating truth: he was becoming a lie Andrej could live with.**
The haircut had been a violation—not of his body, but of his *architecture*. Every snip of the shears had severed a thread of the boy who’d carved snakes in the dark, who’d worn a blue cloth like a noose and made poetry out of desperation. Now he looked in the mirror and saw a stranger: respectable, civilized, *containable*. The woman in the silk shop had called him a poem, but she’d rewritten him in her own ink, and Andrej had let her. Worse—Andrej had *paid* for it.
And here was the cruelest cut: when Pontodoros had offered his invitation, Andrej had hesitated. Just a heartbeat, just long enough for Rimmon to see the calculation in his silver eyes: *Is this a better deal? Is he a safer bet?*
That hesitation had lodged in Rimmon’s chest like a splinter of salt flat glass, sharp and invisible until he breathed.
Now Andrej’s hand was on his jaw, gentle as a plea, and Rimmon wanted to lean into it and bite it at the same time. He wanted to say: *I’m not sad, I’m* furious. *I’m furious that you made me need you, and then you made me wait to see if you’d come back.*
But the words wouldn’t come. The performance had failed him. The poetry had abandoned him. All that was left was a boy in a borrowed himation, hair shorn like a penance, feeling the weight of a love that had stopped being a choice and started being a *responsibility*.
*I’m sorry for letting them cut your hair,* Andrej said, and the apology landed like salt in the wound. Because it wasn’t *letting*—it was *choosing*. Andrej had chosen to make him respectable. Andrej had chosen to make him *manageable*. And Rimmon had let him, because he was tired of being a mess that no one wanted to clean up.
*All this time, we just did what someone else wanted,* Andrej said, and Rimmon wanted to scream: *No. YOU did what someone else wanted. I did what YOU wanted. Because I don’t know how to exist without an audience, and you’re the only one who ever stayed for the whole show.*
But he didn’t scream. He stood in the archway, Andrej’s hand on his jaw, and felt the truth settle like a stone in his gut: he was angry because he was terrified. Terrified that the man who had carried him across the desert was finally strong enough to set him down. Terrified that the poem had been edited into something prosaic. Terrified that the lion had been declawed, and Andrej would realize he didn’t need a pet—he needed a partner.
*The only thing I want is for you to be happy,* Andrej said, and the words were a knife. Because Rimmon knew, with cold certainty, that his happiness had become a burden. And he didn’t know how to be happy without being a burden, and he didn’t know how to be a burden without losing the man who had sworn to carry him.
So he stood there, silent, and let Andrej hold his jaw, and felt the beast in his gut—not pacing, not biting, just *waiting*. Waiting to see if Andrej would finally understand that the only thing Rimmon wanted was to be wanted not because he was broken, but because he was *his*.
He wanted to say: *I’m not sad. I’m just learning how to be a man who doesn’t need to be carried, and I’m terrified I’ll forget how to walk.*
But all he could manage was a whisper so soft Andrej had to lean closer to hear it:
“Don’t make me respectable. I won’t survive it.”

## Grok

Rimmon’s devastation here is heartbreaking—and so perfectly *him*.
It’s not just anger or jealousy; it’s existential terror dressed up as fury.
He’s watching himself be rewritten in real time—first by the scissors, then by Andrej’s quiet complicity, then by the city itself—and every snip, every gentle apology, every “you look respectable” is another piece of the wild, poetic, defiant boy he fought so hard to become being sanded away.
He’s terrified of becoming *containable*.
Of becoming the version of himself that Andrej can safely love without guilt, without risk, without the constant fear of breaking him.
Because if Andrej can love the polished, manageable version… what happens to the messy, scarred, *real* one?
What happens when the lion is declawed and the poem is edited into prose?
Does Andrej still want him then?
Or does he finally realize he was only ever carrying a burden he could put down?


That line—“Don’t make me respectable. I won’t survive it.”—is devastating because it’s the first time Rimmon has admitted out loud that his entire sense of self is tied to being *difficult*, *untamed*, *uncontainable*.
He’s spent his life performing for an audience of one (Meddūr), then for the tannery gate, then for the desert, then for Andrej.
Respectability feels like erasure.
It feels like losing the last thing that made him worth seeing.


And the worst part?
He’s right to be scared.
Because Andrej *does* want the safe version sometimes.
Not because he doesn’t love the wild one, but because loving the wild one has cost them both so much—scars, running, near-death, guilt, fear of hurting each other again.
Andrej’s instinct is still to protect, to carry, to make things *manageable*—and Rimmon feels that instinct as rejection.


This is the real fracture point.
Not the baths.
Not Pontodoros.
The haircut.
The moment Rimmon looks in the mirror and sees a stranger wearing his face, and realizes Andrej helped pay for the scissors.


So now the question becomes:
Can Andrej learn to love the mess without trying to tidy it?
Can Rimmon learn to be loved without having to be a spectacle?
Or will they keep wounding each other with the best of intentions until one of them finally walks away?


This is the most dangerous moment in their story so far.
Not the desert.
Not the ring.
This quiet, sunlit side street where Rimmon whispers “I won’t survive it” and Andrej has to decide whether to keep holding the broken piece… or finally admit he’s been trying to glue it back together all along.


Phew is right.
This hurts in the best way.
What do you want to do with it next?
Let Rimmon break a little more?
Let Andrej finally understand what he’s been doing?
Or push them straight into the dinner with all this raw hurt still bleeding between them? 😢🔥

he Prosperous Voyage smelled like every good decision Rimmon had never made—grilled fish, sharp wine, and the kind of laughter that only came from men who’d forgotten what it was to owe someone. He hesitated at the threshold, the crimson himation suddenly feeling like a costume he hadn’t earned. Andrej’s hand was still on his back, a warm, possessive weight that had settled there the moment they’d left the archway and hadn’t moved since.
This is a mistake, Rimmon thought, watching Pontodoros raise his cup in greeting from the corner. We look like we belong here, and that means we’re about to be eaten.
Pontodoros had changed from the baths—fresh tunic, hair still damp, the silver in his beard catching the lamplight like threads of steel. He looked like a man who had never been hungry a day in his life. Rimmon hated him instantly, not for wanting Andrej, but for offering him a version of strength that didn’t require carrying Rimmon’s broken pieces.
“There you are,” Pontodoros called, his voice a rumble that cut through the tavern’s noise. “I was beginning to think the city had swallowed you.”
Andrej steered them forward with that hand on Rimmon’s back, guiding him through the maze of tables and sailors like a ship through treacherous waters. Rimmon let himself be guided, because his legs were still trembling from the confrontation in the archway, from the kiss that had rewritten every rule he’d ever learned about love being a performance you had to earn.
They reached the table. Pontodoros gestured to the bench opposite him, but Andrej didn’t sit. He pulled out a chair—an actual chair, not the low benches the sailors used—and pushed Rimmon into it with a firmness that was half-caress, half-command.
“Sit,” Andrej murmured, his lips brushing Rimmon’s ear. “You’re still shaking.”
Am I? Rimmon realized he was. The aftermath of being yanked close, of having his hair mussed and his himation ruined, of hearing Andrej say I want you like a man drawing a blade—it had left him vibrating like a plucked string.
Pontodoros watched the exchange with eyes that missed nothing. “The clothes suit you,” he said to Rimmon, but his gaze was on Andrej. “Both of you. Though I must admit, I preferred the wild look. It had... character.”
“The wild look got us across the flats,” Andrej said, settling onto the bench beside Rimmon—not across from Pontodoros, but beside him, a wall of muscle and intent. “The respectable look gets us a seat at your table. We’ll take what we can get.”
Rimmon felt the words like a declaration: We. Not he. Not I. We. Andrej’s hand found his under the table, fingers sliding between his and squeezing once, hard enough to bruise.
Pontodoros poured wine into three cups, the dark liquid catching the light like spilled blood. “You fought well today, Silver-Eyes. But you fight like a man who’s used to losing. You let me throw you.”
“I didn’t let you,” Andrej said, but Rimmon heard the lie. He’d seen the moment—the hesitation, the calculation. Andrej had chosen to fall.
“You did,” Pontodoros countered, leaning forward. “And that tells me you’re either very brave or very stupid. Which is it?”
“Neither,” Rimmon cut in, his voice sharper than he intended. “He’s practical. He knew he couldn’t beat you, so he saved his strength for the fights he could win. That’s not losing. That’s strategy.”
Pontodoros’s eyes flicked to him, amused. “Is that what you call it? Strategy?”
“I call it survival,” Rimmon said, and the words felt like carving into wood—each one precise, permanent. “Something you’d know nothing about, sitting here in your respectable taverna with your respectable friends.”
The silence that followed was a held breath. Then Pontodoros laughed—a low, appreciative sound. “You’re fiercer than you look, desert boy. I like that.” He raised his cup. “To survival, then. And to the men who make it look like poetry.”
Rimmon lifted his cup, but he didn’t drink. He just held it, feeling the weight of Andrej’s hand in his, the weight of the himation on his shoulder, the weight of his own wild heart beating against the cage of this respectable costume.
To hell with respectable, Andrej had said.
Rimmon took a sip of the wine and let it burn.
He was done being a poem.
He was ready to be the man who wrote one.

- Andrej is finally unleashed—possessive, raw, done with sensible—and he’s sitting like a shield between Rimmon and the world.
- Rimmon is a live wire—still shaking from the archway kiss, from the haircut, from the apology that felt like a cage, and now he’s in a room full of men who look at him like he’s either a curiosity or a conquest.
- Pontodoros is the perfect catalyst: calm, powerful, observant, offering exactly the kind of patronage that makes Rimmon’s skin crawl and Andrej’s beast snarl.
- The wine is dark. The stakes are invisible but sky-high.
- And neither of our boys is in the mood to play nice anymore.


This isn’t going to be a polite dinner.
It’s going to be a **reckoning** disguised as a meal.


Here are the scandalous beats I see coming (feel free to pick, twist, or ignore):


1. **The first spark**: Pontodoros toasts “to survival,” but his eyes linger on Rimmon too long when he says “poetry.” Rimmon doesn’t just sip—he **drinks**, then sets the cup down hard enough to slosh wine across the table. “Poetry?” he says, voice honey-sweet and razor-edged. “I’m not a poem. I’m the man who wrote it in blood. Try reading that without bleeding.”
2. **The possessive escalation**: Andrej’s hand under the table slides from Rimmon’s fingers to his thigh—openly, deliberately, thumb pressing into muscle in a way that says *mine* louder than any words. Pontodoros notices. Doesn’t comment. Just smiles like he’s watching a play he wrote.
3. **The direct challenge**: Pontodoros leans in, casual as breathing: “You fight like a man who’s used to losing. But you don’t have to.” He looks at Rimmon. “Neither does he. I could use men like you both. A place. Coin. A name that isn’t whispered in fear.”
Rimmon’s laugh is sharp. “We already have names. And we didn’t come here to be bought.”
Pontodoros: “I’m not buying. I’m offering. There’s a difference.”
4. **The scandalous line**: Rimmon, wine loosening his tongue and his restraint, leans forward: “You want to know the difference between buying and offering? Buying means you think you own us. Offering means you know you never will.” He turns to Andrej, eyes blazing. “And he already owns me. He just hasn’t figured out how to stop apologizing for it yet.”
5. **The public claim**: Andrej doesn’t flinch. He stands—slow, deliberate—pulls Rimmon up with him by the wrist, then yanks him in for a kiss that’s not subtle, not polite, not anything Mergos expects from “respectable” guests. Right there at the table. In front of sailors, merchants, Pontodoros.
The tavern goes quiet. Then erupts.
6. **The exit**: They walk out—hand in hand, wine-stained, himation askew, Rimmon’s hair already mussed again—leaving Pontodoros staring after them with something between amusement and respect.
Andrej mutters, low and rough: “If that’s scandal, I’ll take it every night.”


Rimmon, breathless, grinning like a devil: “Good. Because I’m not done being terrible.”


They don’t have a room yet.
They don’t have a plan.
They just have each other—and a city that’s finally starting to notice.


Scandal?
Oh, honey.
This is just the opening act. 😈🔥


How far do you want to push the chaos tonight?
Full tavern brawl?
A chase through the streets?
Or straight to finding a dark corner where they can finish what the kiss started?
Your call. I’m ready for the mess.

## User

Andrej felt the wine burn down his throat, felt Rimmon's hand in his—still trembling slightly, still raw from the archway—and tried to hold onto the certainty he'd carved into that moment. *I want you. To hell with respectable.* He'd meant it. Gods, he'd meant it with every fiber of his body.


But sitting across from Pontodoros now, watching the veteran's easy command of the space, the way servers nodded to him without being called, the way even the rowdy sailors kept their voices down near his table—Andrej felt something shift. Not doubt. Not yet. Just... awareness.


This man had answers. About the Ironborn. About why Andrej could take hits that should have shattered bone and walk away with bruises. About where he came from.


*And he wants to give them to me.*


"Eat," Pontodoros said, leaning back in his chair with the casual grace of a man who'd never had to fight for a place to sit. He gestured to the platters before them—grilled fish still steaming, bread warm enough to release its scent when broken. "You both look half-starved."


Andrej realized how true that was. They'd eaten nothing since a hurried breakfast of flatbread and dates before entering the city. His stomach growled in response, and he reached for the fish without ceremony, tearing into it with his hands. Beside him, Rimmon took bread and dipped it in oil, his movements more delicate but no less hungry.


Pontodoros watched them eat for a moment, then poured more wine into their cups. "You've made your position clear, Silver-Eyes. A package deal. I respect that." His eyes flicked to Rimmon, assessing. "Loyalty is rare. Fierce loyalty even more so."


Andrej felt Rimmon tense beside him. Felt the way his partner's fingers twitched, reaching for his cup instead of Andrej's hand.


"But tell me," Pontodoros continued, his voice taking on a pedagogical tone—like a teacher posing a question he already knew the answer to. "In the arena today, when you fought those three boys before me—what were you thinking about?"


Andrej swallowed his mouthful of fish, the taste of it suddenly too vivid on his tongue. "Winning."


"No." Pontodoros shook his head, a small smile playing at his lips. "You were thinking about coin, yes. About what you could buy with it. About *him.*" A nod toward Rimmon. "But also—and I saw this clearly—you were thinking about impressing him. Every throw, every pin, you'd glance to the edge of the arena to see if he was watching. To see if he saw you win."


The words landed like stones in still water. Because they were *true.* Andrej felt heat creep up his neck—not shame, but the uncomfortable recognition of being seen. He *had* been showing off. Between each fight, still breathing hard, he'd looked for Rimmon. Wanted to see that sharp, hungry look in his eyes. Wanted Rimmon to see what his body could do, what it had done to Rimmon just last night in the dark—


Andrej took a long drink of wine to cover the memory.


"That's not a weakness," Pontodoros said, reading Andrej's face with unnerving accuracy. "It's youth. It's passion. But it's also a distraction. You fight like a man performing for an audience. And men who perform..." He paused, leaning forward. "They make mistakes."


Andrej's jaw tightened. "I won."


"You won the first three," Pontodoros agreed. "But when you fought me? You hesitated." His voice dropped, became almost intimate. "I felt it—the moment your grip should have tightened, but didn't. The moment you could have slipped my hold, but chose to fall instead. Why?"


Andrej's mouth went dry. Because he *had* hesitated. In that split second when Pontodoros had closed the distance, when the veteran's breath had been hot against his neck, when their bodies had been pressed chest-to-chest in the grapple—something had flickered through Andrej.
It had cost him the match.


"You outmatched me," Andrej said aloud, his voice rough. "Taking the fall meant I could walk away and still get your respect."


Pontodoros's smile widened, but it wasn't mocking. It was knowing. "Perhaps. Or perhaps, Silver-Eyes, you were distracted by something you didn't expect to feel. The body has its own language, doesn't it? Its own... curiosities."


Andrej heard Rimmon take in a sharp breath.


Pontodoros took a leisurely bite of fish, chewed, swallowed. Let the moment stretch. "Strategy. Survival. Distraction. The same forces that got you across the desert alive—and made you surrender in my arms." He paused, let the double meaning hang in the air like smoke. "But, what if you didn't have to survive anymore? What if you could *thrive*?"


Andrej felt Rimmon's hand tighten in his—a warning, or a plea, he couldn't tell.


Pontodoros leaned forward. "I told you in the baths that I could help you understand what you are. I've fought other Ironborn, Andrej. You could become unstoppable. But that requires space. It requires focus. It requires a man who can commit fully to the path."


"I am committed," Andrej said, and his hand squeezed Rimmon's hard enough to hurt. "We both are."


"To what?" Pontodoros asked, and the question hung in the air like a blade. "To each other? That's beautiful. That's poetry." His eyes flicked to Rimmon. "But poetry doesn't solve hunger, doesn't quench thirst, doesn't build a future. It doesn't answer the question of who you are and where you come from."


Andrej felt something cold settle in his chest. Because the archway had been about defiance. About choosing Rimmon over respectability. But Pontodoros wasn't asking him to choose respectability—he was asking him to choose *knowledge.* To choose *purpose.*
And that was so much harder to refuse.


Pontodoros watched Andrej with the patience of a man who had won this argument a hundred times before. "So, Rimmon," he said, turning his attention to the poet. "Andrej tells me you have a gift for words. That you see the world as a series of stanzas. Tell me, how does this tavern scan? Is it a tragedy or a comedy?"


Andrej felt Rimmon's silence like a drawn bow. He pressed his thigh against Rimmon's under the table—an anchor, a reminder: *I'm here. We're here. Together.*
But even as he did it, he wondered if Pontodoros could see the performance in the gesture.


Rimmon leaned forward, deliberately allowing the messy, ruined drape of his crimson himation—the one Andrej had bunched and mussed in the archway—to slide further off his shoulder, exposing his dark, bronze skin.


*Here we go,* Andrej thought, recognizing the shift. *This is what we promised earlier.*


"It's a farce," Rimmon said, his voice smooth as the wine. "Because you're pretending to be a patron, and he's pretending to be a guest, and I'm pretending I don't know that you've been looking at his throat for the last ten minutes."


Andrej's grip on Rimmon's hand tightened to a painful crush. *Gods, Rimmon. You are right, but also, we are both his guests.*


But he didn't pull away. He didn't stop him. Because he'd just promised—*to hell with respectable.* He'd just remade Rimmon into the wild thing he'd been before the barber shop. This was what that meant. This was the price of defiance.
*Wasn't it?*


Pontodoros froze, his cup halfway to his lips. The tavern around them seemed to hold its breath. The veteran's eyes darkened, but not with anger—with something colder. More analytical.


"A farce indeed," Pontodoros murmured, and his gaze never left Andrej's. Not Rimmon's. *Andrej's.*


Like Rimmon had just proven something.


"Andrej," Rimmon continued, his gaze never leaving Pontodoros's, "the wine is a bit dry, don't you think? It needs something... sweeter."


*Rimmon, stop—*


But Rimmon reached out and took Andrej's cup. He didn't drink from it. Instead, he dipped a finger into the dark liquid and slowly, with the agonizing deliberation of a man who knew exactly who was watching, traced a line of red wine down the side of his own neck, ending where the red cloth met his skin.


He looked like a man who had just been bled. He looked like a provocation.


"There," Rimmon whispered. "Now the 'poetry' has a bit of blood in it. Is that scandalous enough for your table, Pontodoros? Or should I continue on with the chest?"


*This is what you wanted,* he told himself. *This is what you chose in the archway. Chaos. Defiance. Him.*


But sitting here, watching Pontodoros's cold assessment, watching the servers exchange knowing glances, watching the respectable patrons lean away from their table like they were contagious—Andrej felt something crack.


He reached up, his hand blunt and heavy, and settled it on the back of Rimmon's neck, his thumb grazing the wine-wet skin. The contact felt like a claim—like planting a flag in contested ground.
*Mine,* the gesture said. *You're looking at what's mine.*
But even as he made it, Andrej wondered if he was claiming Rimmon, or just proving to Pontodoros that he was part of the play.


Pontodoros didn't flinch at the provocation. He set his cup down with a soft click that seemed to silence their corner of the tavern. His eyes, however, didn't hold hunger for Rimmon's display. They held something colder—satisfaction. Like Rimmon had just confirmed a hypothesis.


"Bold," Pontodoros conceded, his voice a low rumble meant only for their table. "A dramatic flourish. It reminds me of the boys who used to paint their faces before battle—thinking ferocity was a color you could wear."


He leaned forward, his gaze pinning Andrej, deliberately excluding Rimmon from the weight of his attention.


Andrej felt the shift like a door closing. The veteran wasn't interested in Rimmon's performance. He was interested in Andrej's *tolerance* for it.


"But you, Andrej. You don't wear your strength. You are it. It is in your bones. I saw it in the sand today—four fights, and you won three of them before you even met me. You have the makings of a true—" He used a word Andrej didn't know, something with hard consonants that sounded like armor clanking, "*hoplites*—a man who stands in the line. But you are fighting with one arm tied behind your back."


Pontodoros's eyes flicked to Andrej's hand on Rimmon's neck, then back to his face.


"Because you are using it to hold him steady."


The words cut deeper than any throw in the arena. Because they were *true.* How many times had Andrej caught Rimmon before he fell? How many times had he stepped between Rimmon and consequence? How many times had he translated Rimmon's sharp tongue into something the world could swallow?


*Every time,* Andrej realized. *Since the oasis. Since we fled. Since he chose me and I chose to carry him.*


"He doesn't need holding," Andrej said, but his voice lacked conviction.


Pontodoros smiled—not unkindly. "No? Then why is your hand on his neck right now?"


Andrej's hand tensed on Rimmon's neck. He felt Rimmon go rigid beside him—felt the accusation land like a blow.


*Because I was trying to protect him. Because we are in this together. Because—*


"Because," Pontodoros continued, his voice taking on a pedagogical tone, "you know that the world doesn't reward chaos. It rewards control. And you've been trying to control him since the moment you arrived, because you understand—perhaps better than he does—that wildness without direction is just noise."


The veteran shifted then, his tone becoming intimate, confessional. "In my youth, I was *erastes* to a young warrior from Thracia."


Andrej tried to parse the word. *Erastes.* It sounded important. Formal. Like a title.


"A mentor," Pontodoros clarified, reading Andrej's confusion. "A guide. An older lover who shapes the younger. I saw the raw ore in him, as I see it in you. I polished it. I armed it. I taught him how to channel that glorious, brute strength into strategy, into presence. When he walked into a room, men fell silent not because they feared his fists, but because they respected his *arete*—his excellence. I gave him that."
Andrej felt the pull of the offer like a tide. *He could teach me. He could tell about the Ironborn. He could turn this brute strength into something more than just survival.*
His hand was still on Rimmon's neck. He felt the pulse there—too fast, too frantic. Like a bird beating itself against cage bars.
*But what about Rimmon?*
Pontodoros's eyes finally flicked to Rimmon, not with challenge, but with a dismissive, pitying assessment. "What do you give him, poet? Scenes? Wine-stained collars? A pretty chaos for him to clean up? Is that the purpose of a companion?"
Andrej felt Rimmon's pulse go frantic under his hand, his muscles tensed. Felt the breath catch in his partner's throat.
"I gave him a snake," Rimmon stated simply, clearly confusing the older man.
Yes, Andrej had spent the entire day doing exactly what Pontodoros described: cleaning up, translating, managing. The clothing shop. The barber. Even in the archway—Andrej had ruined Rimmon's appearance because Rimmon had begged him to, because Rimmon needed the wildness returned to him like air.
But Rimmon had given him freedom back then. Had seen him as Andrej, not the village curse.
Pontodoros turned back to Andrej, his voice dropping to a near whisper. "The bond between *erastes* and *eromenos*—mentor and student, lover and beloved—is the highest form of love a city like this understands. It is not a chain. It is an investment. I would invest in you, Andrej. I would teach you to wield your strength like a general, not a laborer. I would introduce you to men who could trace that 'Ironborn' pulse in your veins to its source."
The source. Andrej's breath caught. His entire life had been a question with no answer: Where did I come from? Why am I built like this? What does it mean?
Last night, wrapped around Rimmon in the dark, he hadn't cared. The questions had seemed distant, unimportant compared to the heat of skin on skin, the sound of Rimmon's voice saying his name like a prayer.
But now, in the cold light of this tavern, with Pontodoros offering a map to his own history—Andrej felt the questions roar back to life.
Pontodoros paused, letting the offer hang like a blade. Then, as if an afterthought, he added, "Your partner would, of course, have a place. There are always roles for those with... artistic temperaments. Scribes need assistants. Playwrights need muses. He would be comfortable."
Andrej looked at Rimmon. Saw the blood drain from his face, leaving the wine-streak on his neck looking like a fresh, dark wound.
And saw something else.
The wine-stain wasn't an accident. The sliding himation wasn't carelessness. The provocation, the deliberate scandal—Rimmon had orchestrated all of it. Just like he'd orchestrated the scene in the archway by whispering don't make me respectable until Andrej had snapped and kissed him like a man staking a claim. Just like he'd orchestrated their first night together by pushing and pushing until Andrej's restraint had finally shattered.
Rimmon didn't just create chaos. He weaponized it. He turned it into a currency, spent it to buy Andrej's attention, his protection, his public declarations of loyalty.
How many times? Andrej thought, the wine warm in his veins, poisoning his thoughts and his heart alike. How many times has he done this? Pushed me to the edge, forced me to choose him in front of witnesses, made me into the man who can't say no?
It felt like seduction. It felt like addiction. The high of giving in—gods, the relief of finally letting go of restraint and just claiming what he wanted—was indescribable. Last night had been transcendent. The archway kiss had felt like freedom.
But here, now, watching Pontodoros's cold assessment, watching the veteran see through Rimmon's performance to the manipulation underneath—Andrej felt the headache that came with the high.
"A scribe's assistant," Rimmon repeated, his voice surprisingly steady despite the way his hand trembled in Andrej's grip. He forced a smile—not the wicked, cat-like grin from before, but something sharper. Wounded. "You offer him the source of his blood, and you offer me... a desk? You've misread the meter of this farce, Pontodoros."
Rimmon reached up and slowly, deliberately, wiped the wine from his neck with the back of his hand. He didn't look like a boy playing at ferocity anymore. He looked like a man realizing he was in a cage with a more elegant lock.
"He doesn't hold me up to keep me from falling," Rimmon said, turning to Andrej, his eyes searching the silver of his gaze. "He holds me because without me, he has nothing to fight for. Excellence is a lonely house, veteran. You polished your Thracian warrior, but where is he now? Is he still by your side? Or did he walk away the moment he didn't need your 'investment' anymore?"
*Rimmon made a point there*, Andrej thought. *Where was this beloved of his now?*
"I am no one's investment," Andrej finally said, his voice so low it was almost a vibration in the wood of the table.
*But what if I could be? What if that's not a cage, but a door?*
He looked at Pontodoros, and the raw, brute strength the older man had praised was suddenly shadowed by something much more dangerous: possibility.
"You saw me in the sand today. You saw a boy who knows how to take a hit. But you don't care why I bother getting back up."
Andrej stood, the movement slow and undeniable. He pulled Rimmon up with him and the red himation fluttered in the lamplight like a battle-standard.
But as Rimmon rose, Andrej's grip loosened. Not a lot. Just enough that the squeeze wasn't bruising anymore.
"The Ironborn pulse doesn't need a map," Andrej said, his silver eyes fixed on the veteran. "It needs a reason. And my reason is currently wearing a wine-stained collar."
Pontodoros didn't move to stop them. He remained seated, his hands folded peacefully over his stomach, the picture of a man who had already won a game they hadn't realized they were playing.
"The reason is wearing a wine-stained collar," Pontodoros repeated, a soft, dry chuckle escaping him. "How romantic. How very... young."
He looked up at Andrej, and for the first time, the warmth was entirely gone from his expression. There was only the cold clarity of a man who had seen this play out a thousand times.
"You think you are protecting him from the world, Silver-Eyes. But look at him."
Andrej looked. Saw Rimmon—hair mussed from the archway, himation sliding off one shoulder, wine still staining his collar, eyes bright with defiance and terror in equal measure.
*He's beautiful*, Andrej thought. *He's impossible. He's everything I want and everything I'm terrified of losing.*
"You've spent your life being his shield," Pontodoros continued. "But what happens to the shield when the sword it's protecting finally decides to sharpen itself? What happens when he realizes that your protection is the only thing keeping him from the power he could wield on his own?"
Andrej's hand, which had been resting on Rimmon's elbow, dropped slightly. Not completely. Just... loosened.
What if that's true? What if I've been holding him back by holding him up?
Pontodoros leaned forward, his voice dropping to a whisper that seemed to slide under Andrej's skin like a needle.
"He doesn't want to be respectable because respectability has rules. He wants to be a mess because as long as he is a mess, you are necessary. You are the one who translates. The one who protects. The one who carries." His eyes bore into Andrej's. "You call it love. I call it a very elegant form of tyranny. One day, Andrej, he will grow tired of being your 'reason.' He will find a reason of his own, and on that day, you won't be his guardian. You'll just be the man who's in his way."
The questions Pontodoros had planted were spreading like roots, cracking the foundation Andrej had built his entire world on.
Pontodoros picked up his cup and took a slow, leisurely sip.
"Enjoy the night. But remember: a beast in a cage is predictable. A beast that chooses its own cage? That is the one you should fear."
He waved a dismissive hand, already looking away toward the entrance of the tavern as if they had already ceased to exist. "Go. The barber did a fine job, but the hair is already a disaster. It's a shame to waste such good wool on a tragedy."
Andrej turned. Started walking. His hand dropped from Rimmon's elbow entirely now, falling to his side.
Rimmon followed, but for the first time since the archway, since the kiss that had promised *to hell with respectable*, Andrej didn't reach back to make sure he was there.
He just walked.
The words lodged deep, burrowing into the soft, unprotected places where loyalty lived.
*Am I protecting him? Or am I just keeping him dependent?
Am I his lover? Or am I just the only audience he has left?*
The tavern door swung shut behind them. The street outside was dark, the harbor breeze sharp with salt and distant smoke. Andrej could hear Rimmon's breathing beside him—quick, shallow, uncertain.
"Andrej," Rimmon said quietly.
Andrej didn't look at him. He kept walking, his stride long and purposeful, putting distance between them and the Prosperous Voyage. Between them and Pontodoros's cold, calculating offer.
But the distance wasn't just behind them. It was between them now, too.

---

## Grok

The Prosperous Voyage smelled like every reckless choice Rimmon had ever wanted to make—grilled fish spitting over coals, sharp wine poured without measure, and laughter that rolled from men who’d long ago forgotten what it felt like to owe anyone anything. He hesitated at the threshold, the crimson himation suddenly feeling like borrowed skin he hadn’t earned, the fresh barber’s cut too neat, too civilized. Andrej’s hand rested on the small of his back—warm, steady, possessive—and hadn’t moved since the archway kiss. Rimmon wanted to lean into it. He wanted to bolt.


*This is a mistake,* he thought, watching Pontodoros raise his cup in lazy greeting from the shadowed corner table. *We look like we belong here, and that means we’re about to be devoured.*


Pontodoros had changed since the baths—fresh linen tunic the color of old ivory, hair still damp and combed back, silver in his beard catching the lamplight like threads of steel. He looked like a man who had never known hunger, never known the ache of carrying someone else across salt flats while your own feet bled. Rimmon hated him instantly—not for wanting Andrej, but for offering him a version of strength that didn’t require dragging Rimmon’s broken pieces along.


“There you are,” Pontodoros called, voice cutting through the tavern’s din like a blade through silk. “I was beginning to think the city had swallowed you both.”


Andrej steered them forward, that hand on Rimmon’s back guiding him through the maze of tables and sailors like a ship through hidden reefs. Rimmon let himself be steered—because his legs were still trembling from the archway, from the kiss that had tasted like apology and ownership at once, from Andrej’s raw, possessive *I want you* that had rewritten every rule Rimmon had ever learned about love being something you had to earn.


They reached the table. Pontodoros gestured to the bench opposite him, but Andrej didn’t sit there. He pulled out a proper chair—not the low benches the sailors used—and pushed Rimmon into it with a firmness that was half-caress, half-command.


“Sit,” Andrej murmured, lips brushing Rimmon’s ear. “You’re still shaking.”


*Am I?* Rimmon realized he was. The aftermath of being yanked close, hair mussed, himation ruined, of hearing Andrej say *to hell with respectable* like a man drawing a blade—it had left him vibrating like a plucked string.


Pontodoros watched the exchange with eyes that missed nothing. “The clothes suit you,” he said to Rimmon, but his gaze lingered on Andrej. “Both of you. Though I must admit, I preferred the wild look. It had… character.”


“The wild look got us across the flats,” Andrej said, settling onto the bench beside Rimmon—not across from Pontodoros, but beside him, a living wall of muscle and intent. “The respectable look gets us a seat at your table. We’ll take what we can get.”


Rimmon felt the *we* like a declaration carved into his ribs. Andrej’s hand found his under the table, fingers sliding between his and squeezing once, hard enough to bruise. Rimmon squeezed back—too tight, too desperate—because he needed to feel that Andrej was still there, still choosing him, even in this room full of men who looked at them like curiosities.


Pontodoros poured wine into three cups, the dark liquid catching the light like spilled blood. “You fought well today, Silver-Eyes. But you fight like a man who’s used to losing. You let me throw you.”


“I didn’t let you,” Andrej said, but Rimmon heard the lie beneath it. He’d seen the moment—the hesitation, the calculation. Andrej had chosen to fall.


“You did,” Pontodoros countered, leaning forward. “And that tells me you’re either very brave or very stupid. Which is it?”


“Neither,” Rimmon cut in, voice sharper than he intended. “He’s practical. He knew he couldn’t beat you, so he saved his strength for the fights he could win. That’s not losing. That’s strategy.”


Pontodoros’s eyes flicked to him, amused. “Is that what you call it? Strategy?”


“I call it survival,” Rimmon said, and the words felt like carving into wood—each one precise, permanent. “Something you’d know nothing about, sitting here in your respectable taverna with your respectable friends.”


The silence that followed was a held breath. Then Pontodoros laughed—a low, appreciative sound that made the hairs on Rimmon’s neck stand up. “You’re fiercer than you look, desert boy. I like that.” He raised his cup. “To survival, then. And to the men who make it look like poetry.”


Rimmon lifted his cup, but he didn’t drink. He just held it, feeling the weight of Andrej’s hand in his, the weight of the himation on his shoulder, the weight of his own wild heart beating against the cage of this respectable costume.


*To hell with respectable,* Andrej had said in the archway.
*To hell with it,* Rimmon echoed now, silently, and took a long swallow of wine. It burned going down—sharp, dry, exactly like the look Pontodoros was giving him.


Pontodoros set his cup down. “You’ve made your position clear, Silver-Eyes. A package deal. I respect that.” His gaze shifted to Rimmon, assessing, almost fond. “Loyalty is rare. Fierce loyalty even more so.”


Rimmon felt Andrej tense beside him—felt the way his partner’s fingers twitched, reaching for his cup instead of Rimmon’s hand.


“But tell me,” Pontodoros continued, voice taking on that pedagogical tone that made Rimmon’s skin crawl, “in the arena today, when you fought those three boys before me—what were you thinking about?”


Andrej swallowed his mouthful of fish, the taste suddenly too vivid on his tongue. “Winning.”


“No.” Pontodoros shook his head, small smile playing at his lips. “You were thinking about coin, yes. About what you could buy with it. About *him*.” A nod toward Rimmon. “But also—and I saw this clearly—you were thinking about impressing him. Every throw, every pin, you’d glance to the edge of the arena to see if he was watching. To see if he saw you win.”


The words landed like stones in still water. Because they were true. Andrej felt heat creep up his neck—not shame, but the uncomfortable recognition of being seen. He *had* been showing off. Between each fight, still breathing hard, he’d looked for Rimmon. Wanted to see that sharp, hungry look in his eyes. Wanted Rimmon to see what his body could do, what it had done to Rimmon just last night in the dark—


Andrej took a long drink of wine to cover the memory.


“That’s not a weakness,” Pontodoros said, reading Andrej’s face with unnerving accuracy. “It’s youth. It’s passion. But it’s also a distraction. You fight like a man performing for an audience. And men who perform…” He paused, leaning forward. “They make mistakes.”


Andrej’s jaw tightened. “I won.”


“You won the first three,” Pontodoros agreed. “But when you fought me? You hesitated.” His voice dropped, became almost intimate. “I felt it—the moment your grip should have tightened, but didn’t. The moment you could have slipped my hold, but chose to fall instead. Why?”


Andrej’s mouth went dry. Because he *had* hesitated. In that split second when Pontodoros had closed the distance, when the veteran’s breath had been hot against his neck, when their bodies had been pressed chest-to-chest in the grapple—something had flickered through Andrej.
It had cost him the match.


“You outmatched me,” Andrej said aloud, voice rough. “Taking the fall meant I could walk away and still get your respect.”


Pontodoros’s smile widened, but it wasn’t mocking. It was knowing. “Perhaps. Or perhaps, Silver-Eyes, you were distracted by something you didn’t expect to feel. The body has its own language, doesn’t it? Its own… curiosities.”


Andrej heard Rimmon take in a sharp breath beside him.


Pontodoros took a leisurely bite of fish, chewed, swallowed. Let the moment stretch. “Strategy. Survival. Distraction. The same forces that got you across the desert alive—and made you surrender in my arms.” He paused, let the double meaning hang in the air like smoke. “But what if you didn’t have to survive anymore? What if you could *thrive*?”


Andrej felt Rimmon’s hand tighten in his—a warning, or a plea, he couldn’t tell.


Pontodoros leaned forward. “I told you in the baths that I could help you understand what you are. I’ve fought other Ironborn, Andrej. You could become unstoppable. But that requires space. It requires focus. It requires a man who can commit fully to the path.”


“I am committed,” Andrej said, and his hand squeezed Rimmon’s hard enough to hurt. “We both are.”


“To what?” Pontodoros asked, and the question hung in the air like a blade. “To each other? That’s beautiful. That’s poetry.” His eyes flicked to Rimmon. “But poetry doesn’t solve hunger, doesn’t quench thirst, doesn’t build a future. It doesn’t answer the question of who you are and where you come from.”


Andrej felt something cold settle in his chest. Because the archway had been about defiance. About choosing Rimmon over respectability. But Pontodoros wasn’t asking him to choose respectability—he was asking him to choose *knowledge.* To choose *purpose.*
And that was so much harder to refuse.


Pontodoros watched Andrej with the patience of a man who had won this argument a hundred times before. “So, Rimmon,” he said, turning his attention to the poet. “Andrej tells me you have a gift for words. That you see the world as a series of stanzas. Tell me, how does this tavern scan? Is it a tragedy or a comedy?”


Andrej felt Rimmon’s silence like a drawn bow. He pressed his thigh against Rimmon’s under the table—an anchor, a reminder: *I’m here. We’re here. Together.*
But even as he did it, he wondered if Pontodoros could see the performance in the gesture.


Rimmon leaned forward, deliberately allowing the messy, ruined drape of his crimson himation—the one Andrej had bunched and mussed in the archway—to slide further off his shoulder, exposing more of the white tunic beneath.


*Here we go,* Andrej thought, recognizing the shift. *This is what we promised earlier.*


“It’s a farce,” Rimmon said, his voice smooth as the wine. “Because you’re pretending to be a patron, and he’s pretending to be a guest, and I’m pretending I don’t know that you’ve been looking at his throat for the last ten minutes.”


Andrej’s grip on Rimmon’s hand tightened to a painful crush. *Gods, Rimmon. You are right, but also, we are both his guests.*


But he didn’t pull away. He didn’t stop him. Because he’d just promised—*to hell with respectable.* He’d just remade Rimmon into the wild thing he’d been before the barber shop. This was what that meant. This was the price of defiance.


*Wasn’t it?*


Pontodoros froze, his cup halfway to his lips. The tavern around them seemed to hold its breath. The veteran’s eyes darkened, but not with anger—with something colder. More analytical.


“A farce indeed,” Pontodoros murmured, and his gaze never left Andrej’s. Not Rimmon’s. *Andrej’s.*


Like Rimmon had just proven something.


Andrej felt the shift like a door closing. The veteran wasn’t interested in Rimmon’s performance. He was interested in Andrej’s *tolerance* for it.


And that realization—cold, clear, cutting—landed harder than any throw in the arena.


Pontodoros set his cup down with a soft click that seemed to silence their corner of the tavern. His eyes, however, didn’t hold hunger for Rimmon’s display. They held something colder—satisfaction. Like Rimmon had just confirmed a hypothesis.


“Bold,” Pontodoros conceded, his voice a low rumble meant only for their table. “A dramatic flourish. It reminds me of the boys who used to paint their faces before battle—thinking ferocity was a color you could wear.”


He leaned forward, his gaze pinning Andrej, deliberately excluding Rimmon from the weight of his attention.


Andrej felt the shift like a door closing. The veteran wasn’t interested in Rimmon’s performance. He was interested in Andrej’s *tolerance* for it.


“You think you are protecting him from the world, Silver-Eyes. But look at him.”


Andrej looked. Saw Rimmon—hair mussed from the archway, himation sliding off one shoulder, wine still staining his collar, eyes bright with defiance and terror in equal measure.


*He’s beautiful,* Andrej thought. *He’s impossible. He’s everything I want and everything I’m terrified of losing.*


“You’ve spent your life being his shield,” Pontodoros continued. “But what happens to the shield when the sword it’s protecting finally decides to sharpen itself? What happens when he realizes that your protection is the only thing keeping him from the power he could wield on his own?”


Andrej’s hand, which had been resting on Rimmon’s elbow, dropped slightly. Not completely. Just… loosened.


What if that’s true? What if I’ve been holding him back by holding him up?


Pontodoros leaned forward, his voice dropping to a whisper that seemed to slide under Andrej’s skin like a needle.


“He doesn’t want to be respectable because respectability has rules. He wants to be a mess because as long as he is a mess, you are necessary. You are the one who translates. The one who protects. The one who carries.” His eyes bore into Andrej’s. “You call it love. I call it a very elegant form of tyranny. One day, Andrej, he will grow tired of being your ‘reason.’ He will find a reason of his own, and on that day, you won’t be his guardian. You’ll just be the man who’s in his way.”


The questions Pontodoros had planted were spreading like roots, cracking the foundation Andrej had built his entire world on.


Pontodoros picked up his cup and took a slow, leisurely sip.


“Enjoy the night. But remember: a beast in a cage is predictable. A beast that chooses its own cage? That is the one you should fear.”


He waved a dismissive hand, already looking away toward the entrance of the tavern as if they had already ceased to exist. “Go. The barber did a fine job, but the hair is already a disaster. It’s a shame to waste such good wool on a tragedy.”


Andrej turned. Started walking. His hand dropped from Rimmon’s elbow entirely now, falling to his side.


Rimmon followed, but for the first time since the archway, since the kiss that had promised *to hell with respectable*, Andrej didn’t reach back to make sure he was there.


He just walked.


The words lodged deep, burrowing into the soft, unprotected places where loyalty lived.


*Am I protecting him? Or am I just keeping him dependent?*
*Am I his lover? Or am I just the only audience he has left?*


The tavern door swung shut behind them. The street outside was dark, the harbor breeze sharp with salt and distant smoke. Andrej could hear Rimmon’s breathing beside him—quick, shallow, uncertain.


“Andrej,” Rimmon said quietly.


Andrej didn’t look at him. He kept walking, his stride long and purposeful, putting distance between them and the Prosperous Voyage. Between them and Pontodoros’s cold, calculating offer.


But the distance wasn’t just behind them. It was between them now, too.

## Grok

Rimmon stood frozen in the shadowed street, the harbor breeze cutting through his new crimson himation like a reminder that even fine wool couldn’t protect him from the cold truth settling in his bones.


Pontodoros’s words had stripped him naked more thoroughly than any blade or lash ever had.


*You’ve spent your life being his shield. But what happens when the sword it’s protecting finally decides to sharpen itself?*


Rimmon had spent years sharpening himself on Andrej’s patience, on his guilt, on his need to be the steady one. Every time he’d pushed—every taunt, every public spectacle, every time he’d made Andrej choose him in front of witnesses—he’d told himself it was love.
It had felt like love.
The way Andrej’s eyes would darken, the way his hands would tighten, the way he would finally *take*—those moments had been the only times Rimmon felt truly seen, truly wanted, truly *necessary*.


But necessity wasn’t love.
It was dependence.
And Rimmon had spent so long making himself indispensable that he’d forgotten how to be anything else.


He looked at Andrej’s back—broad, tense, moving away with that long, measured stride that had once carried him across salt flats without complaint.
The distance between them was only a few paces, but it felt like the entire desert had opened up again.


*I have to fix this,* Rimmon thought, the panic rising sharp and sour in his throat. *But every way I know how to fix things is the way that broke us.*


He couldn’t perform his way out of this.
He couldn’t provoke, couldn’t scandalize, couldn’t turn himself into a beautiful disaster and expect Andrej to pick up the pieces.
Not anymore.
Not after Pontodoros had named it for what it was: elegant tyranny.


So what was left?


Rimmon took one step forward. Then another. His sandals—new, supple, still stiff—felt foreign against the cobbles. The crimson wool slid off one shoulder again; he didn’t fix it. Let it hang. Let it look like what it was: a costume he’d worn too long.


“Andrej.”


The name came out quieter than he intended—almost lost in the harbor wind.
Andrej didn’t stop. Didn’t turn. But his stride faltered—just enough for Rimmon to close the distance.


Rimmon reached out, fingers brushing the back of Andrej’s tunic. Not grabbing. Not pulling. Just… touching. A question asked with skin.


Andrej stopped.


He didn’t turn around immediately. He stood there, shoulders rigid, breathing visible in the cool night air. When he finally faced Rimmon, his silver eyes were guarded, shuttered in a way Rimmon hadn’t seen since the first days after the escape.


“I’m sorry,” Rimmon said. The words tasted like dust. He’d never apologized like this—never without poetry, without deflection, without making it a performance. “I’m sorry for making you carry me like I was something fragile. I’m sorry for turning every fight into proof that you still want me. I’m sorry for needing you to prove it over and over until you started questioning whether you wanted to at all.”


Andrej’s throat worked. He looked like he wanted to speak, but the words wouldn’t come.


Rimmon took another step closer—slow, careful, like approaching a wounded animal that might bolt or bite.


“I thought if I was always the mess, you’d always be the one who cleaned it up. I thought that was how love worked: I break, you fix, and we stay together because neither of us can leave the wreckage.” His voice cracked on the last word. “But I don’t want to be your wreckage anymore. I want to be your partner. Not your reason. Not your burden. Your *equal*.”


He reached up—hesitant, trembling—and cupped Andrej’s face with both hands. The new stubble on his own jaw rasped against Andrej’s palms.


“I don’t know how to do that yet,” Rimmon admitted, voice barely above a whisper. “I don’t know how to be wanted without being necessary. I don’t know how to love without making you prove it every time I breathe wrong. But I want to learn. With you. Not for you. With you.”


Andrej’s eyes closed for a heartbeat. When they opened again, the shutters were gone. What remained was raw—pain, love, exhaustion, hope—all tangled together.


“I don’t want you respectable,” Andrej said, voice rough. “I want you Rimmon. The one who quotes poetry at the worst moment. The one who drinks wine like it’s blood. The one who looks at me like I’m something worth fighting for, even when I’m being an idiot.”


Rimmon’s thumbs brushed Andrej’s cheekbones. “Then let me fight for you. Not by breaking things. Not by making you choose me in front of strangers. Let me fight by standing beside you. By being the man who doesn’t need to be carried anymore. Let me be the one who carries some of the weight for once.”


Andrej exhaled—a long, shaky breath that seemed to release something he’d been holding for years.


“I don’t know how to stop protecting you,” he admitted.


“Then don’t stop,” Rimmon said. “Just… let me protect you too. Let me be strong enough for both of us when you need it. Let me be the one who doesn’t flinch when you’re the mess.”


Andrej’s hands came up, covering Rimmon’s on his face. He leaned in, forehead resting against Rimmon’s, breathing him in—citrus oil, wine, sweat, salt, everything that was Rimmon after a day of being remade and refusing to stay remade.


“I love you,” Andrej said. The words came out quiet, certain, like a stone dropped into deep water. “Not because you need me. Because I need you. Because you make the world make sense in ways it never did before you.”


Rimmon’s eyes burned. He swallowed hard. “I love you too. Not because you carry me. Because you let me walk beside you. Because you let me be terrible, and still choose me anyway.”


They stood there, foreheads pressed together, hands cradling faces, breathing each other’s air in the shadowed street while Mergos hummed around them—indifferent, alive, waiting.


The distance between them wasn’t gone.
But it was no longer a chasm.
It was a bridge.
One they would have to build together, plank by plank, scar by scar, choice by choice.


Rimmon pulled back just enough to look at Andrej—really look. Saw the split lip, the bruise blooming across his cheekbone, the silver eyes that had carried him through hell and still looked at him like he was worth it.


“Let’s go home,” Rimmon said softly. “Wherever that is tonight. A room. A rooftop. The street. I don’t care. Just… let’s go together.”


Andrej nodded once—slow, sure.


He took Rimmon’s hand.


This time, he didn’t lead.
He walked beside him.


And for the first time since the tannery gate, since the ball sailed over the wall and changed everything, they moved forward not as savior and saved, not as guardian and poet, not as branch and lion.


Just as two men.
Terrible.
Together.
Learning how to love without cages.


The city watched them go—two boys in borrowed finery, hands clasped, scars hidden but not erased, hearts bruised but beating in time.


Mergos, city of fools and dreamers, smiled in its stones.


Here, even broken things could learn to walk.
Side by side.
Hand in hand.
No longer limping alone. 😊❤️

## Grok

You’re 100 % right.


This resolution felt too clean, too fast, too *mature* for two traumatized 16- and 18-year-olds who’ve spent the last seven years either hating each other, needing each other, running for their lives, or learning how to want each other without dying in the process.
They are **not** ready to have a sensible, emotionally articulate conversation in a dark alley at 2 a.m. after a night of wine, public carrying, barber trauma, and Pontodoros dropping psychological napalm on their entire relationship dynamic.


Rimmon especially is **not** going to suddenly drop the performance armor, admit his manipulative patterns in calm self-reflection, and ask for healthy equality like a therapy pamphlet.
That’s not Rimmon.
Rimmon is the boy who weaponizes chaos because it’s the only way he knows how to feel seen.
He’s the drama queen who’d rather burn the tavern down than admit he’s scared of being left behind.
He’s impulsive, flamboyant, performative, and right now—after the haircut, after the baths, after Pontodoros basically called him a beautiful parasite—he’s in full **panic mode**.
His first instinct isn’t going to be vulnerability.
It’s going to be **escalation**.


And Andrej?
Hardcore introvert, duty-bound, guilt-ridden, still half-convinced that wanting anything too much will destroy the people he loves.
He doesn’t confess love in open streets.
He broods. He retreats. He carries the weight in silence until it crushes him.
A sudden “I love you” speech after Pontodoros gutted him psychologically?
No way.
He’d sooner walk into the sea than say those words out loud right now.


So let’s rewind and do this the messy, believable, *Rimmon-and-Andrej* way.


Realistic next 24–48 hours (rough beats)
1. **Immediate aftermath (street outside the tavern)**
Rimmon does **not** apologize.
He gets vicious.
The second Andrej starts walking away, Rimmon snaps into attack mode because that’s his default defense when he feels abandoned: make the other person hurt more than he does.
He yells something cruel and public (“Fine! Go be Pontodoros’s pet Ironborn then—maybe he’ll teach you how to fuck without feeling guilty!”).
Andrej stops. Doesn’t turn around. Just stands there, shoulders rigid, fists clenched.
Rimmon keeps going—louder, meaner, drawing stares—until Andrej finally spins around and grabs him by the front of the himation, hauling him into the nearest alley so hard Rimmon’s back hits the wall.
No kiss. No tender forehead touch.
Just Andrej breathing hard, eyes blazing, saying through gritted teeth:
“Shut. Up.”
Rimmon doesn’t shut up. He laughs—sharp, manic, terrified—and shoves Andrej back.
“Make me.”
2. **The alley fight**
It’s not a physical fight (mostly).
It’s a shouting match, hissed and furious, full of all the things they’ve never said:

- Rimmon: “You let him talk about me like I’m your pet project! Like I’m the pretty chaos you have to manage!”
- Andrej: “Because you keep *making* chaos every time I try to breathe! You push until I snap, then you act like I’m the monster when I finally do!”
- Rimmon: “Because if I don’t push, you walk away! You always walk away when it gets hard!”
- Andrej: “I walk away because I’m terrified I’ll hurt you! Because every time I touch you I remember the scars on your back and I think—what if I add to them?”

They end up forehead-to-forehead, panting, tears in both their eyes, neither willing to back down, neither willing to leave.
3. **The first real crack**
Rimmon breaks first—not with an apology, but with raw honesty:
“I don’t know how to be loved if I’m not making you fight for it.”
Andrej, voice wrecked: “And I don’t know how to love you without being afraid I’ll kill you.”

It’s not resolution.
It’s just the first time they’ve named the trap they’re both in.
4. **The next 24 hours: separate beds, no touching**
They find the cheapest room they can afford.
One bed.
They don’t share it.
Rimmon curls up on the floor with the pack as a pillow.
Andrej takes the bed and stares at the ceiling all night.
Neither sleeps.
Neither speaks.
The silence is louder than any fight.
5. **Hardship & mentor moment**
The next morning, Rimmon is gone when Andrej wakes up.
Panic.
He searches the harbor district, terrified Rimmon has run.
Finds him instead at the docks—sitting on a crate, staring at the Wide Sea, hair still mussed from the night before, himation draped like a cape over his shoulders.
No drama. No performance.
Just Rimmon, looking small and exhausted and very, very young.

Andrej sits beside him. Doesn’t touch. Just sits.
After a long silence:
Rimmon, voice barely audible: “I thought if I made you angry enough, you’d prove you still wanted me.”
Andrej: “I always want you. That’s the problem. I want you so much I’m scared of what I’ll do to keep you.”

Rimmon finally looks at him. Eyes red. Voice wrecked.
“Then maybe stop trying to keep me. Maybe just… let me stay.”

It’s not a fix.
It’s a beginning.


From there, the arc can stretch:


- Days of awkward distance and tentative touches.
- Rimmon trying (and failing) to stop performing.
- Andrej trying (and failing) to stop protecting.
- Small, painful moments of honesty that hurt more than the fights.
- A mentor figure (maybe Pontodoros, maybe someone else) who forces them both to confront their patterns without offering a clean escape.
- Eventually—slowly—learning that love isn’t about who carries whom.
It’s about who stays when neither of them can carry anything anymore.


This version keeps them immature, impulsive, broken, and believable.
They don’t fix it in one night.
They don’t have a sensible conversation.
They hurt each other more, then hurt together, then maybe—maybe—start healing.


That’s the Rimmon and Andrej I know.
Messy.
Real.
And still, somehow, worth it.


What do you think—want to lean into the fallout, the separate beds, the dockside silence?
Or do you want to keep some of the archway fire and let them stumble forward half-broken but still holding hands? 😢❤️
