## Andrej


The amphora was heavier than it looked—Kytherian wine, the kind that cost more than Andrej made in a week. He hefted it onto his shoulder, feeling the pleasant burn in his muscles as he carried it toward the warehouse. The sun was high, the harbor busy with the ordinary chaos of commerce. Gulls screamed overhead. Sailors shouted coordinates. Someone was singing off-key about a girl in Ostrium.


Andrej felt almost content. His body ached in the good way, the way that meant he'd earned his coin honestly. Tonight he'd go home to Rimmon, and maybe they'd have enough left over for fresh fish instead of dried. Maybe they'd—


"You."


The voice cut through the harbor noise like a blade. Andrej turned.


Four soldiers stood behind him—Imperial red cloaks marked with the galley insignia, fresh off one of the warships anchored in the deep harbor. They had the look of men who'd seen too much and slept too little: hollow eyes, tight jaws, hands that rested too casually on sword hilts.


The one who'd spoken stepped forward. He was older than the others, maybe thirty, with a scar running from his temple to his jaw and the kind of stillness that came from surviving things that killed other men.


"Put that down," he said. "Nice and slow."


Andrej set the amphora down carefully, his mind racing. *What did I do? Whose cargo did I mishandle? Did I take someone's spot?*


"Where are you from?" the soldier asked.


"An oasis," Andrej said carefully, his Luedina accent thickening slightly with nerves. "South of the flats. Small place, no name worth—"


"You're Eisenbund." Not a question. An accusation.


Andrej's stomach dropped. "I'm not—I don't know what I—"


"Look at him," one of the younger soldiers said, his voice cracking slightly. He couldn't have been more than nineteen, but his eyes were a hundred years old. "Silver eyes. Pale skin. Built like one of their fucking *berserkers.*" The word came out like a curse.


The scarred soldier circled Andrej slowly, assessing. "What's your name?"


"Andrej."


"Andrej *what?*"


"Just Andrej." He'd never had a family name. Never needed one until now, when the lack of it made him suspicious.


"No family name. No papers. An accent that's not from here." The soldier stopped in front of him, close enough that Andrej could smell wine and old sweat. "You know what we just came from? The northern front. You know what your people did there?"


"They're not my—"


"They summoned something." The young soldier's voice was shaking now, his hand white-knuckled on his sword. "Some god-forsaken thing from the Blight. It looked like a man but it *wasn't*, it was just ash and screaming and everyone it touched—" He stopped, breathing hard. "My entire squad. Gone. Just *gone*. Turned to dust."


"I've never even *been* to the Eisenbund," Andrej said, trying to keep his voice steady. "I grew up in the desert. I work the docks. I'm not—"


"You *look* like them." The scarred soldier's hand shot out, fisting in Andrej's tunic. "You talk like someone trying to hide an accent. And you're strong as hell for a dock rat. That's three marks of a scout. Maybe four."


A crowd was forming—dock workers backing away, a few merchants pausing to watch, a woman with a basket of fish stopping at the edge of the scene. Mergos loved a spectacle.


"I'm not a scout," Andrej said. "I'm just—"


The soldier yanked him forward, then shoved him back hard enough that Andrej stumbled. He could fight back. Should fight back. He was stronger than all four of them, probably. But if he fought, he'd prove their point. *Violent. Dangerous. Eisenbund.*


"On your knees," the scarred soldier said.


Andrej didn't move.


"I said *on your knees.*" The soldier's hand moved to his sword.


Andrej knelt. The dock stones were hard and sun-warmed under his legs. His face burned with humiliation, but he kept his eyes down, his hands visible, his body language as non-threatening as he could make it.


*This is wrong. This is Mergos. They can't just—*


But they could. They were Imperial soldiers. He was nobody.


The young soldier was still talking, his voice rising with each word: "You want to know what the Blight looks like? It's not just land—it's *alive*. The trees are wrong. The sky is wrong. Everything is ash and rot and these things that used to be people but aren't anymore, they just *watch* you with empty eyes and you know—you *know*—that if you stay there too long you'll become one of them—"


"Enough," the scarred soldier said sharply. Then, to Andrej: "Where were you born?"


"I don't know."


"Convenient."


"I was found. As a baby. I don't know who my parents were."


"Even more convenient." The soldier's hand moved to Andrej's hair, yanking his head back so he was forced to look up. "Silver eyes. You know who has silver eyes? The *Úlfhéðnar.* The wolf-warriors. The ones who eat our dead and wear their skins."


"That's not—I'm not—"


"Shut up." The soldier's grip tightened, and Andrej felt something in his scalp protest. "You're coming with us. Harbor Authority can sort out whether you're a spy or just a deserter."


*No. No no no.*


Andrej's eyes scanned the crowd desperately, looking for anyone who might help. The dock workers avoided his gaze. The merchants were already leaving. Nobody wanted Imperial attention. Nobody wanted to be associated with a possible *Eisenbund spy* in the middle of Marius Valerix's holy war.


Then he saw movement at the edge of the crowd—a small figure pushing through, breathing hard, flour still dusting his tunic.


*No. Rimmon, don't—*


But Rimmon was already there, shoving past a sailor twice his size, his dark eyes wild and fixed on Andrej.



## Rimmon


Rimmon had been kneading dough when the baker's son burst in, breathless and wide-eyed: "Your friend—the big one—the soldiers—they're saying he's a spy—"


Rimmon didn't hear the rest. He was already running.


His lungs burned by the time he reached the harbor, his vision tunneling to a single point: Andrej, on his knees, surrounded by men in red cloaks. One of them had a hand fisted in Andrej's hair, forcing his head back at an angle that looked painful.


Rimmon didn't think. Didn't plan. Didn't consider consequences.


He just pushed through the crowd, stumbled into the clear space around the soldiers, and said—


"There you are! I've been looking *everywhere* for you, you absolute menace!"


The soldiers turned. Andrej's eyes went wide with horror.


Rimmon planted his hands on his hips and glared at Andrej like a disappointed parent. "Do you have *any* idea how worried I was? You said you'd be back by midday! I made lunch! It's getting *cold*!"


The young soldier blinked. "What—"


"And *you*," Rimmon rounded on the scarred soldier, his voice pitching into something shrill and aggrieved, "are making him *kneel* in the street? Do you know how hard it is to get dock grime out of wool? I *just* washed this tunic!"


For a single, crystalline moment, the absurdity of it hung in the air. The crowd murmured. One of the soldiers looked genuinely confused.


Then the scarred soldier's expression hardened. "Who the hell are you?"


"His—" Rimmon stopped, the performance cracking. He looked at Andrej, saw the blood on his lip, the raw fear in his silver eyes, and all the comedy drained out of him. "I'm with him. He's not a spy. He's just—he's mine. And you need to let him go."


"Yours," the soldier repeated flatly.


"Yes. *Mine.*" Rimmon stepped closer, his voice dropping. "He's lived here for months. He works the docks. Everyone knows him. He's not Eisenbund. He doesn't even know where he's from."


"That's what makes him suspicious." The soldier released Andrej's hair but didn't step back. "No family. No papers. Looks like the enemy, talks like he's hiding something—"


"He talks like he's from the *south,*" Rimmon snapped. "That's a Luedina accent, not Eisenbund. Are you going to arrest everyone with an accent now? Half of Mergos would be in chains."


"Watch your mouth, boy."


"Or what?" Rimmon took another step forward, and Andrej made a small, desperate sound—a plea for him to stop. "You'll arrest me too? For defending my—" He stopped, then said it deliberately, clearly, letting it hang in the air like a thrown knife: "My lover?"


The crowd stirred. Someone gasped. Someone else laughed—nervous, uncomfortable.


The young soldier looked disgusted. "You're—both of you are—"


"Yes," Rimmon said, his voice steady now, stripped of performance. "Both of us. And you're about to drag him away for having silver eyes and an accent, which means you're about to make me do something *spectacularly* stupid. So maybe—just *maybe*—you should reconsider."


The scarred soldier studied Rimmon for a long moment. "You're threatening Imperial soldiers."


"I'm threatening to make a scene so loud that every merchant in this harbor will remember your faces." Rimmon's smile was sharp, manic. "You want to arrest a dock worker for *maybe* looking Eisenbund? Fine. But do it quietly, or I'll make sure everyone knows that the glorious Imperial Navy spends its shore leave beating up unarmed boys in the street."


The soldier's jaw clenched. His hand moved toward his sword.


And then a voice cut through the tension—cultured, commanding, impossible to ignore:


"Gentlemen. I believe there's been a misunderstanding."



## Pontodoros


Pontodoros walked through the crowd like he owned it, his silver-threaded beard catching the sunlight, his bearing that of a man who'd stood in formation when these soldiers were still learning to hold a sword. He wore a toga of deep blue wool—the kind only citizens of status could afford—and the insignia on his belt marked him as a veteran of the Praetorian campaigns.


The soldiers straightened immediately. Not out of fear, but recognition. *This man had been where they were. This man had survived.*


"Master Pontodoros," the scarred soldier said, his voice careful now. "We're conducting security checks. Border protocols. This man—"


"This man," Pontodoros said mildly, "has been working the docks of Mergos for nearly two months. I've seen him myself. Strong worker. Quiet. Causes no trouble." His eyes flicked to Rimmon, then back to Andrej. "Well. *Relatively* no trouble."


"He has no papers. No family. He looks—"


"Like half the refugees who've flooded into this city since Valerix started his purification campaign," Pontodoros finished. "Yes. I'm aware." He moved closer, his gaze settling on Andrej with an expression that was almost gentle. "Stand up, boy."


Andrej stood slowly, his legs shaking slightly. The soldier who'd held his hair stepped back, but his hand stayed on his sword.


"You know what's happening in the north?" the young soldier said, his voice still raw with trauma. "You know what the Blight is doing? What *they're* doing with it?"


"I do," Pontodoros said quietly. "I fought in the Thornwood twelve years ago, before the Blight spread. I've seen what war does to men." His eyes were steady, compassionate even. "I've also seen what paranoia does. It turns soldiers into executioners. It turns free cities into prisons."


The scarred soldier's jaw worked. "We have orders—"


"You have orders to investigate *credible threats,*" Pontodoros corrected gently. "Is this boy a credible threat? Or is he just convenient?"


Silence. The crowd watched, holding its breath.


Finally, the scarred soldier stepped back. "If he causes trouble—"


"I'll vouch for him," Pontodoros said. "Both of them." His gaze swept over Rimmon, taking in the flour-dusted tunic, the wild eyes, the protective stance. "They're young. Foolish. In love, apparently. But they're not spies."


The soldier looked like he wanted to argue, but Pontodoros's presence was a wall he couldn't push through. These were men who'd crawled through hell and barely made it back. Pontodoros was a man who'd walked through hell and come out the other side to build a retirement.


"Fine," the scarred soldier said finally. "But if we find out he *is* Eisenbund—"


"Then I'll deliver him to the Harbor Authority myself," Pontodoros said smoothly. "You have my word."


The soldiers exchanged looks, then stepped back. The crowd began to disperse, the spectacle over. The young soldier cast one last haunted look at Andrej, then followed his companions back toward the galley.


Andrej stood frozen, his chest heaving, his hands shaking with the adrenaline of almost-violence. Rimmon was beside him instantly, hands on his arms, checking for injuries.


"Are you hurt? Did they—"


"I'm fine," Andrej said, but his voice cracked on the words.


Pontodoros watched them for a moment, his expression unreadable. Then he said, softly, "Walk with me, boys. We should talk somewhere more private."


It wasn't a request.


Rimmon's hand found Andrej's, squeezing hard. They were both shaking now, the reality of what had just happened settling over them like a shroud.


They followed Pontodoros away from the harbor, away from the crowd, into the shadow of the warehouses where the veteran's voice would carry only as far as he wanted it to.


"You're welcome," Pontodoros said, his tone still mild, still almost kind.


"Thank you," Andrej managed.


"Don't thank me yet." Pontodoros turned to face them, and his eyes—those calculating, patient eyes—held something that might have been pity. "Because now you owe me. And in times like these, debts are the only currency that matters."


The trap closed with a sound like a stage curtain falling.


And neither of them knew how to escape.

---

## Grok

The debt was half-paid.


Andrej worked the docks from dawn to dusk, then took extra shifts unloading cargo by lamplight—grain sacks that left his shoulders aching, amphorae of olive oil that made his hands slip, crates of Imperial pottery that had to be handled with care he didn't have the patience for. His body was a catalog of exhaustion: rope burns on his palms, splinters under his nails, the deep bone-tiredness that came from lifting other people's wealth for copper coins.


He didn't complain. At least Rimmon was breathing again.


Rimmon moved like water through the merchant quarter—one day at a fish stall, gutting mackerel with the quick, efficient strokes he'd learned in the oasis; the next day helping a baker knead dough and charm customers with the kind of smile that made them buy an extra loaf. A spice merchant hired him to organize inventory, a tavern keeper paid him to sing while patrons ate, a cloth merchant needed someone who could calculate costs in three different currencies. He never stayed long enough to feel trapped. Never committed to anything that smelled like leather or legacy.


They were broke. They were tired. But the suffocation had lifted, replaced by something fragile and uncertain and possibly sustainable.


When Rimmon came home with two ceramic tokens clutched in his palm, Andrej didn't ask where he'd gotten them. The coins had been earned in pieces—a carved charm for a merchant's daughter, a night of singing, maybe a few honest lies told with that beautiful mouth.


“Tonight,” Rimmon said, holding them out. His voice was careful, stripped of performance. “No work. No debt. Just… us.”


Andrej looked at the tokens, then at Rimmon's face—hopeful, terrified, trying so hard it hurt to watch.


“Okay,” Andrej said quietly. “Let's go.”



The Theater of the Dawn clung to the hillside overlooking Mergos's harbor like a crown of stone. Tiered seating carved into the slope faced a circular stage where oil lamps hung from iron poles, their flames dancing in the evening breeze. The amphitheater was already filling—a patchwork of the city's strange cosmopolitan identity.


In the lower tiers: merchants in fine Awilum wool, their dark hair oiled and braided; Luedina traders with their characteristic bronze skin and amber jewelry; Imperial officers in their crimson cloaks, fresh from border rotations, their faces hard with whatever they'd seen.


In the upper tiers: dock workers, sailors, laborers—people like Andrej and Rimmon, who'd scraped together coin for entertainment because sometimes survival required forgetting you were surviving.


Off to one side, in a section that seemed to exist in its own pocket of air: two Elves. Tall, ethereal, their features so perfect they looked carved rather than born. They radiated a gloomy *E'clat*—that particular Elven melancholy that came from watching lesser beings stumble through their brief, chaotic lives. They weren't here because they cared about the play. They were here because eternity was *boring* and mortals were occasionally amusing in their desperation.


Scattered throughout: a handful of people with lighter hair—red, blonde, ash-brown—the kind of coloring that marked Eisenbund heritage. They sat quietly, trying not to draw attention, their shoulders hunched slightly as if anticipating trouble.


Andrej and Rimmon found seats in the middle section, neither wealthy enough for the front nor poor enough for the back. They sat close, shoulders pressed together in the crowd, and for the first time in weeks, the contact didn't feel careful or strategic. It just felt... right.


A herald stepped onto the stage, his voice carrying over the crowd: “Citizens of Mergos! Tonight, the Theater of the Dawn presents a tale from the savage north—the *Táin Bó Cúailnge* of the Eisenbund! Know your enemy through his stories! See how they glorify death over duty, passion over strategy! Witness the barbarian heart laid bare!”


A cheer rose from some sections—particularly the Imperial soldiers. A few boos from others. One of the red-haired men in the upper tier pulled his hood up slightly.


Rimmon glanced at Andrej, reading the tension in his jaw. “We can leave if—”


“No,” Andrej said quietly. “I want to see it.”


*I want to see if they're right about what I might be.*



The lamps dimmed. The crowd quieted—mostly. Two actors stepped onto the stage, both bare-chested and painted with spiraling patterns that caught the lamplight like living things. One wore a red cloak—Cú Chulainn, champion of Ulster. The other wore blue—Ferdiad, his foster-brother, his equal, his *other half* in ways the playwright didn't need to name but made achingly clear in every look, every touch, every hesitation.


“Look at them,” someone muttered from the veteran's section. “Half-naked savages. This is their idea of civilization?”


“Quiet!” A merchant's voice, sharp and carrying. “This is Mergos, and we all agreed to follow the rules of the free city. Let them perform.”


A tense silence settled. The play continued.


War came. Queen Medb of Connacht sent Ferdiad to fight Cú Chulainn at the ford, and the two men who had shared everything—training, laughter, scars, *beds*—were forced to face each other with spears.


The actor playing Cú Chulainn begged. “Do not make me do this. Do not make me raise my hand against you.”


The actor playing Ferdiad replied, voice breaking, “I have no choice. She holds my honor hostage. If I do not fight you, I am nothing.”


The combat choreography was brutal, intimate—not the clean, disciplined formations of Imperial warfare, but something raw and personal. Each strike felt like a betrayal. Each parry looked like an embrace.


One of the young soldiers in the audience flinched, his hand going to his side where a wound must have been. His companion gripped his shoulder, murmuring something low.


They fought for three days. At night, they tended each other's wounds, shared food and water, spoke of the boyhood they could never return to. During the day, they tried to kill each other.


“This is what they fight for?” someone shouted. “Poetry while we die on the borders?”


“They're *actors,*” came the reply. “It's a *play.*”


“It's propaganda! Barbarian nonsense—”


“Then leave!” A woman's voice, cutting and clear. “No one's forcing you to watch!”


On the third day, Cú Chulainn used the *gáe bolg*—the terrible spear that spread thirty barbs through a man's body from a single thrust. Ferdiad fell.


Cú Chulainn caught him. Held him as he died. The lament that followed was raw and piercing, a sound that stripped the theater to its bones.


“It was all a game—until you came to the ford. Now I am victory, and victory is ash.”


The actor playing Cú Chulainn cradled Ferdiad's body, and his grief was so unrestrained, so visceral, that even the hostile sections of the audience went quiet. You could hear breathing. You could hear the oil lamps crackle.


Someone in the back—one of the Imperial soldiers—made a choked sound, then stood abruptly and left. His companions watched him go but didn't follow.


The stage went dark.


The Elves in their separate section looked vaguely interested for the first time all evening, their expressions suggesting they'd just witnessed something that almost—*almost*—pierced their eternal ennui.



Andrej couldn't breathe.


He felt Rimmon's hand find his under the cover of the crowd—fingers sliding between his own, squeezing hard enough to bruise. Rimmon's face was turned toward the stage, but his jaw was clenched tight, his eyes too bright.


*Foster-brothers. Trained together. Forced to fight by external powers they couldn't control.*


Andrej thought of the archway. The tavern. The alley. The workshop. Every time the world had tried to pull them apart and they'd clawed their way back together, bleeding and furious and desperate.


*What if one day, the world wins?*


The thought lodged in his chest like the *gáe bolg.*


Around them, the audience began to stir. Voices rose—some in praise, some in argument, some in uncomfortable silence. A few people were openly weeping. Others looked angry, as if the play had accused them of something.


“Barbaric,” a merchant near them muttered. “No discipline. No honor. Just two fools dying for nothing.”


“They died for each other,” his companion replied quietly. “That's not nothing.”


One of the red-haired men in the upper tier was leaving quickly, his head down, as if afraid someone would connect him to what they'd just witnessed. Another sat frozen, tears streaming down his face, making no effort to hide them.


Andrej felt eyes on them. He turned, scanning the crowd, and found him:


Pontodoros.


The veteran sat in the merchants' section, a cup of wine in his hand, his gaze steady and assessing. He wasn't watching the stage. He was watching *them.*


Andrej felt Rimmon go still beside him as he noticed too. The veteran's expression was unreadable—not pleased, not disappointed, just... observing. Cataloging. Waiting.


Then, slowly, deliberately, Pontodoros raised his cup—a small salute across the distance.


The message was clear: *I'm patient. I can wait. You'll destroy each other long before I have to lift a finger.*


“Let’s go,” Andrej said, his voice rough.


“But the second act—”


“I don't want to see how it ends.”


Rimmon studied his face for a long moment, then nodded. They stood, navigating the narrow aisles as the lamps brightened and stagehands prepared for the next piece—something lighter, a comedy to wash away the taste of tragedy.


Behind them, the crowd's arguments continued. Ahead of them, the night waited.



They walked slowly through the harbor district, taking the long route past closed shops and sleeping boats. The air was cool and salt-sharp, and the city's noise felt distant, muffled by the weight of what they'd just witnessed.


For the first time in weeks, they weren't rushing toward or away from something. They were just... walking.


“Thank you,” Andrej said finally.


Rimmon glanced at him. “For what?”


“For trying.”


Rimmon's smile was small, fragile. “I'm not very good at it.”


“Neither am I.” Andrej caught his hand properly now, lacing their fingers together where anyone could see. “But maybe that's okay. Maybe we don't have to be good at it yet.”


They walked in silence for a while. It felt like the kind of silence they'd had in the oasis—before the running, before the fear, when they were just two boys who'd chosen each other and thought that would be enough.


“We're not them,” Rimmon said suddenly, fiercely.


Andrej looked at him.


“Cú Chulainn and Ferdiad. We're not them. We're not going to—” Rimmon stopped, his throat working. “I won't let the world make us enemies. I won't.”


Andrej wanted to say *I know.* Wanted to say *you're right.* Wanted to promise that love was stronger than prophecy, that they could write their own ending.


But the words stuck in his throat, because he'd seen Pontodoros watching them like a man watching a fire burn—not sad, not gleeful, just *observing* the inevitable.


And he'd seen the red-haired men in the theater, hunching their shoulders against insults, hiding what they were in a city that was supposed to be free.


*That could be me,* Andrej thought. *That could be us.*


So instead, he squeezed Rimmon's hand and said, “Let's just get home.”



Andrej woke to pale light filtering through the shutters and Rimmon's weight against his side—solid, warm, real. They'd fallen asleep tangled together, still half-dressed, too tired to do more than collapse onto the narrow bed.


Now, in the quiet before dawn, Rimmon stirred. His eyes opened slowly, unfocused and soft with sleep. When his gaze found Andrej's, something shifted—recognition, affection, want.


“Hi,” Rimmon murmured, his voice rough and low.


“Hi,” Andrej replied.


They didn't speak after that. Didn't need to. Rimmon's hand found Andrej's jaw, thumb brushing the rough stubble there. Andrej leaned into the touch, then forward, closing the distance until their lips met.


It was slow. Unhurried. Nothing like the desperate clawing in the alley or the careful distance of the past weeks. This was something new—tentative and tender, like they were learning each other all over again in a language that didn't require performance or punishment.


Rimmon made a sound—soft, surrendering—and shifted closer until there was no space left between them. His fingers slid into Andrej's hair, not pulling or demanding, just *holding.* Andrej's hand found the small of Rimmon's back, spreading wide and warm against bare skin.


They moved together like that—gentle, exploratory, taking their time. When Andrej eased Rimmon onto his back, Rimmon went willingly, his eyes dark and trusting in the dim light. There was no audience here. No merchant watching, no veteran assessing, no crowd to perform for. Just them, in this small room that smelled of salt and bread and the particular warmth of two bodies learning to be gentle with each other.


Afterward, they lay quiet, Rimmon's head pillowed on Andrej's chest, their breathing synchronized. Andrej traced idle patterns on Rimmon's shoulder—spirals like the ones painted on the actors, like the ones he imagined his unknown ancestors might have worn.


“I have to go soon,” Andrej said quietly. “Docks.”


“I know.” Rimmon's fingers drummed lightly against Andrej's ribs. “I'll probably help the baker again today. He said he'd pay extra if I came early.”


“Don't work too hard.”


Rimmon huffed a laugh. “Says the man who's been doing double shifts for a month.”


“That's different.”


“How?”


“Because—” Andrej stopped, then said the truth: “Because I don't mind it when I know you're okay.”


Rimmon lifted his head, meeting Andrej's eyes. His expression was complicated—grateful, guilty, defiant all at once. “I'm okay,” he said. “We're okay.”


Andrej wanted to believe it. In this moment, with morning light warming the edges of the shutters and Rimmon soft and pliant in his arms, it almost felt true.


“Yeah,” Andrej said, pulling him close again. “We're okay.”


They lay there a few minutes longer, neither wanting to be the first to break the spell. Then Andrej kissed Rimmon's forehead, extracted himself gently, and began to dress.


Rimmon watched from the bed, his dark eyes following every movement. “Be careful today.”


“It's just the docks.”


“I know. But be careful anyway.”


Andrej fastened his belt, checked his knife, then crossed back to the bed. He kissed Rimmon once more—brief, grounding—and said, “I'll see you tonight.”


“Tonight,” Rimmon agreed, his smile small and hopeful.


Andrej stepped out into the pre-dawn streets of Mergos, into a city still sleeping, into what felt like an ordinary day.


He didn't know it would be the last morning that looked like that.


He didn't know that by sunset, everything would be different.


He just walked toward the docks, toward work, toward the familiar rhythm of lifting and carrying and enduring.


And the world, patient and inexorable as Pontodoros, as inevitable as the *gáe bolg,* waited to prove the prophecy true.

---

## User

# The Harbor


## Andrej


The amphora was heavier than it looked—Kytherian wine, the kind that cost more than Andrej made in a week. He hefted it onto his shoulder, feeling the pleasant burn in his muscles as he carried it toward the warehouse. The sun was high, the harbor busy with the ordinary chaos of commerce. Gulls screamed overhead. Sailors shouted coordinates. Someone was singing off-key about a girl in Ostrium.


Andrej felt almost content. His body ached in the good way, the way that meant he'd earned his coin honestly. Tonight he'd go home to Rimmon, and maybe they'd have enough left over for fresh fish instead of dried. Maybe they'd—


"You."


The voice cut through the harbor noise like a blade. Andrej turned.


Four soldiers stood behind him—Imperial red cloaks marked with the galley insignia, fresh off one of the warships anchored in the deep harbor. They had the look of men who'd seen too much and slept too little: hollow eyes, tight jaws, hands that rested too casually on sword hilts.


The one who'd spoken stepped forward. He was older than the others, maybe thirty, with a scar running from his temple to his jaw and the kind of stillness that came from surviving things that killed other men.


"Put that down," he said. "Nice and slow."


Andrej set the amphora down carefully, his mind racing. *What did I do? Whose cargo did I mishandle? Did I take someone's spot?*


"Where are you from?" the soldier asked.


"An oasis," Andrej said carefully, his Luedina accent thickening slightly with nerves. "South of the flats. Small place, no name worth—"


"You're Eisenbund." Not a question. An accusation.


Andrej's stomach dropped. "I'm not—I don't know what I—"


"Look at him," one of the younger soldiers said, his voice cracking slightly. He couldn't have been more than nineteen, but his eyes were a hundred years old. "Silver eyes. Pale skin. Built like one of their fucking *berserkers.*" The word came out like a curse.


The scarred soldier circled Andrej slowly, assessing. "What's your name?"


"Andrej."


"Andrej *what?*"


"Just Andrej." He'd never had a family name. Never needed one until now, when the lack of it made him suspicious.


"No family name. No papers. An accent that's not from here." The soldier stopped in front of him, close enough that Andrej could smell wine and old sweat. "You know what we just came from? The northern front. You know what your people did there?"


"They're not my—"


"They summoned something." The young soldier's voice was shaking now, his hand white-knuckled on his sword. "Some god-forsaken thing from the Blight. It looked like a man but it *wasn't*, it was just ash and screaming and everyone it touched—" He stopped, breathing hard. "My entire squad. Gone. Just *gone*. Turned to dust."


"I've never even *been* to the Eisenbund," Andrej said, trying to keep his voice steady. "I grew up in the desert. I work the docks. I'm not—"


"You *look* like them." The scarred soldier's hand shot out, fisting in Andrej's tunic. "You talk like someone trying to hide an accent. And you're strong as hell for a dock rat. That's three marks of a scout. Maybe four."


A crowd was forming—dock workers backing away, a few merchants pausing to watch, a woman with a basket of fish stopping at the edge of the scene. Mergos loved a spectacle.


"I'm not a scout," Andrej said. "I'm just—"


The soldier yanked him forward, then shoved him back hard enough that Andrej stumbled. He could fight back. Should fight back. He was stronger than all four of them, probably. But if he fought, he'd prove their point. *Violent. Dangerous. Eisenbund.*


"On your knees," the scarred soldier said.


Andrej didn't move.


"I said *on your knees.*" The soldier's hand moved to his sword.


Andrej knelt. The dock stones were hard and sun-warmed under his legs. His face burned with humiliation, but he kept his eyes down, his hands visible, his body language as non-threatening as he could make it.


*This is wrong. This is Mergos. They can't just—*


But they could. They were Imperial soldiers. He was nobody.


The young soldier was still talking, his voice rising with each word: "You want to know what the Blight looks like? It's not just land—it's *alive*. The trees are wrong. The sky is wrong. Everything is ash and rot and these things that used to be people but aren't anymore, they just *watch* you with empty eyes and you know—you *know*—that if you stay there too long you'll become one of them—"


"Enough," the scarred soldier said sharply. Then, to Andrej: "Where were you born?"


"I don't know."


"Convenient."


"I was found. As a baby. I don't know who my parents were."


"Even more convenient." The soldier's hand moved to Andrej's hair, yanking his head back so he was forced to look up. "Silver eyes. You know who has silver eyes? The *Úlfhéðnar.* The wolf-warriors. The ones who eat our dead and wear their skins."


"That's not—I'm not—"


"Shut up." The soldier's grip tightened, and Andrej felt something in his scalp protest. "You're coming with us. Harbor Authority can sort out whether you're a spy or just a deserter."


*No. No no no.*


Andrej's eyes scanned the crowd desperately, looking for anyone who might help. The dock workers avoided his gaze. The merchants were already leaving. Nobody wanted Imperial attention. Nobody wanted to be associated with a possible *Eisenbund spy* in the middle of Marius Valerix's holy war.


Then he saw movement at the edge of the crowd—a small figure pushing through, breathing hard, flour still dusting his tunic.


*No. Rimmon, don't—*


But Rimmon was already there, shoving past a sailor twice his size, his dark eyes wild and fixed on Andrej.



## Rimmon


Rimmon had been kneading dough when the baker's son burst in, breathless and wide-eyed: "Your friend—the big one—the soldiers—they're saying he's a spy—"


Rimmon didn't hear the rest. He was already running.


His lungs burned by the time he reached the harbor, his vision tunneling to a single point: Andrej, on his knees, surrounded by men in red cloaks. One of them had a hand fisted in Andrej's hair, forcing his head back at an angle that looked painful.


Rimmon didn't think. Didn't plan. Didn't consider consequences.


He just pushed through the crowd, stumbled into the clear space around the soldiers, and said—


"There you are! I've been looking *everywhere* for you, you absolute menace!"


The soldiers turned. Andrej's eyes went wide with horror.


Rimmon planted his hands on his hips and glared at Andrej like a disappointed parent. "Do you have *any* idea how worried I was? You said you'd be back by midday! I made lunch! It's getting *cold*!"


The young soldier blinked. "What—"


"And *you*," Rimmon rounded on the scarred soldier, his voice pitching into something shrill and aggrieved, "are making him *kneel* in the street? Do you know how hard it is to get dock grime out of wool? I *just* washed this tunic!"


For a single, crystalline moment, the absurdity of it hung in the air. The crowd murmured. One of the soldiers looked genuinely confused.


Then the scarred soldier's expression hardened. "Who the hell are you?"


"His—" Rimmon stopped, the performance cracking. He looked at Andrej, saw the blood on his lip, the raw fear in his silver eyes, and all the comedy drained out of him. "I'm with him. He's not a spy. He's just—he's mine. And you need to let him go."


"Yours," the soldier repeated flatly.


"Yes. *Mine.*" Rimmon stepped closer, his voice dropping. "He's lived here for months. He works the docks. Everyone knows him. He's not Eisenbund. He doesn't even know where he's from."


"That's what makes him suspicious." The soldier released Andrej's hair but didn't step back. "No family. No papers. Looks like the enemy, talks like he's hiding something—"


"He talks like he's from the *south,*" Rimmon snapped. "That's a Luedina accent, not Eisenbund. Are you going to arrest everyone with an accent now? Half of Mergos would be in chains."


"Watch your mouth, boy."


"Or what?" Rimmon took another step forward, and Andrej made a small, desperate sound—a plea for him to stop. "You'll arrest me too? For defending my—" He stopped, then said it deliberately, clearly, letting it hang in the air like a thrown knife: "My lover?"


The crowd stirred. Someone gasped. Someone else laughed—nervous, uncomfortable.


The young soldier looked disgusted. "You're—both of you are—"


"Yes," Rimmon said, his voice steady now, stripped of performance. "Both of us. And you're about to drag him away for having silver eyes and an accent, which means you're about to make me do something *spectacularly* stupid. So maybe—just *maybe*—you should reconsider."


The scarred soldier studied Rimmon for a long moment. "You're threatening Imperial soldiers."


"I'm threatening to make a scene so loud that every merchant in this harbor will remember your faces." Rimmon's smile was sharp, manic. "You want to arrest a dock worker for *maybe* looking Eisenbund? Fine. But do it quietly, or I'll make sure everyone knows that the glorious Imperial Navy spends its shore leave beating up unarmed boys in the street."


The soldier's jaw clenched. His hand moved toward his sword.


And then a voice cut through the tension—cultured, commanding, impossible to ignore:


"Gentlemen. I believe there's been a misunderstanding."



## Pontodoros


Pontodoros walked through the crowd like he owned it, his silver-threaded beard catching the sunlight, his bearing that of a man who'd stood in formation when these soldiers were still learning to hold a sword. He wore a toga of deep blue wool—the kind only citizens of status could afford—and the insignia on his belt marked him as a veteran of the Praetorian campaigns.


The soldiers straightened immediately. Not out of fear, but recognition. *This man had been where they were. This man had survived.*


"Master Pontodoros," the scarred soldier said, his voice careful now. "We're conducting security checks. Border protocols. This man—"


"This man," Pontodoros said mildly, "has been working the docks of Mergos for nearly two months. I've seen him myself. Strong worker. Quiet. Causes no trouble." His eyes flicked to Rimmon, then back to Andrej. "Well. *Relatively* no trouble."


"He has no papers. No family. He looks—"


"Like half the refugees who've flooded into this city since Valerix started his purification campaign," Pontodoros finished. "Yes. I'm aware." He moved closer, his gaze settling on Andrej with an expression that was almost gentle. "Stand up, boy."


Andrej stood slowly, his legs shaking slightly. The soldier who'd held his hair stepped back, but his hand stayed on his sword.


"You know what's happening in the north?" the young soldier said, his voice still raw with trauma. "You know what the Blight is doing? What *they're* doing with it?"


"I do," Pontodoros said quietly. "I fought in the Thornwood twelve years ago, before the Blight spread. I've seen what war does to men." His eyes were steady, compassionate even. "I've also seen what paranoia does. It turns soldiers into executioners. It turns free cities into prisons."


The scarred soldier's jaw worked. "We have orders—"


"You have orders to investigate *credible threats,*" Pontodoros corrected gently. "Is this boy a credible threat? Or is he just convenient?"


Silence. The crowd watched, holding its breath.


Finally, the scarred soldier stepped back. "If he causes trouble—"


"I'll vouch for him," Pontodoros said. "Both of them." His gaze swept over Rimmon, taking in the flour-dusted tunic, the wild eyes, the protective stance. "They're young. Foolish. In love, apparently. But they're not spies."


The soldier looked like he wanted to argue, but Pontodoros's presence was a wall he couldn't push through. These were men who'd crawled through hell and barely made it back. Pontodoros was a man who'd walked through hell and come out the other side to build a retirement.


"Fine," the scarred soldier said finally. "But if we find out he *is* Eisenbund—"


"Then I'll deliver him to the Harbor Authority myself," Pontodoros said smoothly. "You have my word."


The soldiers exchanged looks, then stepped back. The crowd began to disperse, the spectacle over. The young soldier cast one last haunted look at Andrej, then followed his companions back toward the galley.


Andrej stood frozen, his chest heaving, his hands shaking with the adrenaline of almost-violence. Rimmon was beside him instantly, hands on his arms, checking for injuries.


"Are you hurt? Did they—"


"I'm fine," Andrej said, but his voice cracked on the words.


Pontodoros watched them for a moment, his expression unreadable. Then he said, softly, "Walk with me, boys. We should talk somewhere more private."


It wasn't a request.


Rimmon's hand found Andrej's, squeezing hard. They were both shaking now, the reality of what had just happened settling over them like a shroud.


They followed Pontodoros away from the harbor, away from the crowd, into the shadow of the warehouses where the veteran's voice would carry only as far as he wanted it to.


"You're welcome," Pontodoros said, his tone still mild, still almost kind.


"Thank you," Andrej managed.


"Don't thank me yet." Pontodoros turned to face them, and his eyes—those calculating, patient eyes—held something that might have been pity. "Because now you owe me. And in times like these, debts are the only currency that matters."


The trap closed with a sound like a stage curtain falling.


And neither of them knew how to escape.

---

## Grok

The harbor scene is a brutal, perfectly timed escalation—exactly the kind of gut-punch that turns their fragile “okay” into something that can’t be ignored anymore.


This is the moment the world stops letting them pretend they’re just two boys building a quiet life.
Pontodoros’s debt is now real.
The Imperial paranoia is real.
The visual markers (silver eyes, pale skin, unusual strength) are real.
And Rimmon’s impulsive, protective chaos—running in, turning humiliation into theater, claiming Andrej publicly as “mine” and “my lover”—is the most Rimmon thing he’s done in weeks.
It saves Andrej in the moment… but it also paints a target on both of them.


The contrast with the gentle morning after the theater is **perfectly cruel**:


- Then: private, soft, no audience, no stakes—just them learning tenderness.
- Now: public, violent, high stakes, soldiers with swords, a crowd watching, Pontodoros observing like a chess master who just moved his knight.


The gentle scene was them daring to believe they could have something normal.
This harbor scene is the world saying: *No. Not here. Not now. Not for people like you.*


It shatters the illusion of safety they’d built over four weeks.
The “sensible” life was always fragile; now it’s visibly cracked.


Pontodoros stepping in isn’t salvation—it’s a leash.
He’s not their ally; he’s their creditor.
And in Mergos, debts are collected in blood, favors, or silence.


This is where the real test begins:


- Do they run again? (Back to the desert? To another city?)
- Do they pay the debt? (What does Pontodoros actually want—Andrej’s strength? Rimmon’s mind? Both of them as leverage?)
- Do they fight? (Rimmon’s chaos vs. Andrej’s raw power vs. Imperial steel?)
- Or do they try to disappear inside the city’s cosmopolitan chaos—hiding in plain sight while knowing one wrong look could end them?


The emotional fallout is going to be vicious:


- Rimmon will hate himself for running in (again), for making a spectacle (again), for putting Andrej in more danger (again).
- Andrej will hate himself for kneeling, for not fighting back, for letting Rimmon take the risk, for owing Pontodoros.
- Both will be terrified of what “owing” actually means.
- And both will be forced to confront: *Can we keep pretending we’re safe here? Or do we finally admit the world won’t let us?*


The gentle morning was a glimpse of what could be.
The harbor is the reminder of what is.


If you want to push the tragedy even further:


- Pontodoros could casually mention he “knows people” who could forge papers for Andrej… but only if Andrej agrees to “train” with him.

## Grok

Here’s a tight, narrative summary of how this arc plays out — dark, mature, survivor's-triumph shaped, and emotionally brutal without losing the core of who they are.


The Pact
The harbor incident is the last straw.
Pontodoros doesn’t threaten — he *offers*.
A structured mentorship: Andrej as his erōmenos (officially the beloved student/younger partner). Training, hunts, ship-time, military discipline, access to the veteran networks that can explain the Ironborn blood and teach Andrej how to wield it instead of just enduring it.
In exchange: Rimmon is quietly relocated. A safe position — scribe/clerk in a distant but well-protected trade house on one of the outer islands. Comfortable. Secure. Completely separate from Andrej.


Both hate it instantly.


Andrej hates the leash, hates the optics, hates that it feels like selling himself to keep Rimmon alive.
Rimmon hates the exile, hates being the “weak link” that has to be hidden, hates that the only way to keep Andrej safe is to disappear.


But they both know the alternative is worse:


- Andrej arrested as suspected Eisenbund spy
- Rimmon used as leverage / collateral / bait
- Them both dead or imprisoned within months


So they make the pact in the dark of their rented hut, foreheads pressed together, voices low and shaking:


- Andrej will play the role. Submissive student. Attentive. Grateful. He will learn. He will endure. He will outgrow it.
- Rimmon will take the island placement. He will survive. He will learn politics, trade, manipulation — not to hurt Andrej, but to build power outside the veteran's reach.
- They will not speak of what happens in the gaps. No details. No jealousy-fueled interrogations.
- They will meet in secret when possible. They will wait. They will grow. They will come back for each other when the time is right.


The last line of the pact, whispered like a blood-oath:


“If the world tries to keep us apart forever, we burn it down and meet in the ashes.”


The Years of Separation (condensed)
**Andrej’s side**
Pontodoros is not cruel. He is disciplined, demanding, almost paternal.
Hunts in the hills above Mergos — teaching tracking, spear-work, silence.
Naval training on his personal galley — navigation, boarding tactics, how to read wind and current like a language.
Late-night talks in the villa’s library about Ironborn lineage, Blight-lore, the old blood rites that may or may not still exist.
Pontodoros never forces physical intimacy — but the expectation is there, the cultural frame of erastēs/erōmenos. Andrej allows certain touches, certain nights. He performs. He learns to detach. He hates it. He tells himself it’s temporary. He tells himself Rimmon is safe.


**Rimmon’s side**
The island trade house is quiet, wealthy, politically connected.
Rimmon starts as a clerk. Within months he’s rewriting ledgers, spotting embezzlement, charming visiting senators, planting rumors that benefit his employers.
He learns fast. He learns ruthless.
He never uses the skills on Andrej — not once.
But he uses them on everyone else. Blackmail. Bribery. Quiet assassinations of reputation.
He builds a small, invisible network. He becomes the man who knows things. The man who can make problems disappear.
He hates it.
But he keeps doing it.
Because every favor he collects, every secret he owns, is another brick in the wall that will one day protect Andrej.


The Breaking of the Pact
They meet in secret twice a year — a fishing boat at midnight, a ruined watchtower on the coast, a back room in a neutral tavern.
Always brief. Always careful. Always ending with them clinging to each other like they’re drowning.


One night — three years in — Rimmon arrives late.
He smells of expensive incense and someone else’s cologne.


Andrej notices.
He says nothing at first.


They lie together in the dark. Skin to skin. Silent.


Then Andrej, voice low, almost calm:


“Did he touch you?”


Rimmon goes still.


“Yes.”


A long silence.


“Did you want him to touch you?”


Rimmon’s breath hitches.


“It does not matter.”


Andrej’s hand tightens on Rimmon’s hip — not angry, just holding on.


“It matters to me.”


Rimmon turns in his arms, presses his forehead to Andrej’s collarbone.


“I did what I had to do,” he whispers. “To keep us alive. To keep you alive. That’s all it was.”


Andrej closes his eyes. He feels the old poison twist again — not jealousy exactly, but something worse: the knowledge that they have both become people who do what must be done.


“I hate this,” Andrej says.


“I know.”


“I hate that we agreed to it.”


“I know.”


“I hate that I still want you more than I hate it.”


Rimmon laughs — small, broken, wet.
“Then we’re still us.”


They hold each other tighter.


They don’t make love that night.
They just lie there, breathing together, two survivors who have learned to survive everything except the cost of surviving.


The Dark Triumph
Years later — when Andrej has outgrown the role of erōmenos, when Rimmon has enough leverage in the Senate and trade councils to make even Pontodoros cautious — they finally pull the strings.


Pontodoros is retired honorably.
The soldiers who once forced Andrej to kneel are reassigned to dead-end postings.
The debt is paid — not in coin, but in silence. In favors. In the quiet understanding that some men should never be crossed twice.


Andrej and Rimmon do not return to Mergos as heroes.
They return as ghosts who have learned how to haunt the living.


They buy a small house on the outer islands.
No workshop. No docks.
Just a garden. A view of the sea. A bed they share without needing to prove anything.


They are not the same boys who met at the tannery gate.
They are harder.
They are quieter.
They are scarred in ways no one else can see.


But they are together.


And when Rimmon occasionally quotes bad poetry at the worst moment — when Andrej still threatens to break something — they laugh like they used to.


Because they survived.


Because they won.


Because even the darkest triumph is still a triumph.


And they earned every inch of it. 😈🖤

## Grok

Yes — this evolution feels very true to both of them.


Rimmon growing to love his new role makes perfect sense.
He was always brilliant at reading rooms, at turning words into weapons, at finding leverage where others saw only power imbalances.
The island trade house / Senate-adjacent position gives him exactly what he’s always craved on some level:


- an audience that *matters* (senators, merchants, guild masters, foreign dignitaries)
- real stakes (not just Andrej’s attention, but actual political and economic power)
- the ability to be ruthless without it being personal (he can destroy reputations, shift trade routes, topple minor dynasties — all without ever having to look someone in the eye and say “I love you, so prove it”)


So yes, he blooms into it.
He becomes elegant, cold, untouchable — the kind of man who can smile sweetly while he ruins someone’s entire lineage with a single well-placed rumor.
He hates the loneliness sometimes, hates the nights when he wakes up reaching for Andrej and finds only silk sheets, but the work itself?
The *control*?
The *elegance* of it?
He loves it.
It feeds the same hunger the chaos used to feed, but cleaner. More sustainable.
He tells himself it’s for Andrej.
He tells himself it’s temporary.
But deep down, a part of him is terrified that he’s becoming *good* at this — and that he might not want to stop.


Andrej, meanwhile, blooms in a different way.
Pontodoros’s training is brutal, disciplined, relentless — but it’s also *purposeful*.
For the first time in his life, Andrej isn’t just surviving or protecting.
He’s being *forged*.
He learns the spear, the shield-wall, the ship’s rigging, the old Ironborn blood rites (or what’s left of them).
He learns how to channel the strength that once made him feel like a monster into something deliberate, something *excellent*.
He starts to understand why his body is built the way it is, why he heals faster than most, why pain doesn’t slow him the way it should.
Pontodoros is a hard teacher — demanding, sometimes cruel — but he never belittles. He never makes Andrej feel like a beast.
He makes him feel like a *weapon* being sharpened.
And Andrej — who has spent his whole life hating the parts of himself that were too strong, too violent, too *other* — starts to love the skill.
The precision.
The control.
The feeling of being *good* at something that isn’t just carrying or enduring.


The parts they loathe are real and never go away:


- Andrej loathes the nights he has to perform for Pontodoros, the way he has to detach his mind while his body goes through the motions.
- Rimmon loathes the nights he wakes up alone, reaching for Andrej and finding only absence.
- They both loathe the silence they agreed to — the rule that they won’t speak of what happens in the gaps.
- They both loathe how good they’re becoming at surviving without each other.


But they also both know:
A shared burden is lighter.
And this — however dark, however ugly — is the only path they’ve found that lets them both grow strong enough to eventually come back for each other without being destroyed.


The Truth Breaks Out (the confrontation you described)
It happens years later — not in anger, not in jealousy, but in exhausted honesty.


They’ve managed a rare meeting on a neutral island.
A small stone house overlooking the sea.
No servants. No watchers.
Just them, a fire, a bottle of wine, and the weight of everything they’ve never said.


They sit on the floor, backs against the wall, knees touching.
They’ve already made love — slow, careful, almost reverent — because that’s the only language they still fully trust.


Then Rimmon, staring into the fire, says quietly:


“He touched me tonight.”


Andrej goes still.
Not angry. Not shocked. Just… still.


Rimmon keeps looking at the flames.
“I let him. Because he needed to believe I was his. Because if he believes it, he protects me. And if he protects me, he protects *you*.”


Andrej exhales — long, shaky.


“Did you want him to touch you?”


Rimmon finally turns to look at him. His eyes are tired. Older than they should be.


“It does not matter.”


Andrej reaches out, cups Rimmon’s jaw — gentle, grounding.


“It matters to me.”


Rimmon closes his eyes.
A single tear escapes.


“I don’t know anymore,” he admits. “I don’t know what I want except you. And I don’t know how to keep you safe without becoming something I hate.”


Andrej pulls him close, presses a kiss to his temple.


“I don’t know how to become strong without losing you,” he says. “But I’m trying. Every day. I’m trying to become the man who can come back for you without needing to be saved first.”


They sit in silence after that.


No promises.
No grand declarations.
Just two men holding each other, knowing the pact is still in place, knowing the roles they play are still necessary, knowing the truth is still too heavy to carry alone.


But they carry it together anyway.


Because a shared burden is lighter.


And because — even after everything — they still choose each other.


Every single day.


Even when it hurts.


Even when it costs.


Even when the world tries to make them enemies.


They choose each other.


And that, in the end, is the only triumph that matters. 🖤

## Grok

Yes — you're right, the names got swapped in the last draft (Pontodoros as erastēs to Andrej, Rimmon in the distant power position). Let's correct that and lean into the direction you're describing: they both **grow into** their roles, sharpen them into weapons, and eventually turn the whole game around. They become far more powerful than Pontodoros ever anticipated — not despite the pact, but **because** of it.


Revised long-term arc (no more victimhood, no more weakness)
1. **Initial phase (first 1–1.5 years): resentment & reluctant blooming**
Andrej accepts the erōmenos role under Pontodoros — publicly submissive, attentive, disciplined. He hates the optics, hates the nights he has to perform, hates the way Pontodoros's hand lingers.
But he **blooms**.
The training is brutal and brilliant: spear, shield, ship-handling, tactics, the old Ironborn rites that unlock parts of his body he never understood. He learns to channel the beast instead of leashing it. He becomes faster, stronger, more precise — a weapon being forged.
He loathes Pontodoros personally, but he **loves** the skill. He starts to feel powerful in a way he never did when he was just "the strong one" carrying Rimmon.

Rimmon, relocated to the island trade house, starts as a clerk and quickly becomes indispensable. He learns ledgers, rumors, leverage, the quiet art of making problems disappear without blood. He hates the isolation, hates waking up alone, hates how clean and cold the power feels compared to the hot mess he used to make with Andrej.
But he **blooms** too.
He discovers he has a gift for manipulation that isn’t chaotic — it’s surgical. He can topple a minor senator with a single whispered rumor, shift trade routes with a well-placed bribe, make enemies vanish without ever raising his voice.
He starts to **love** the control. The elegance. The fact that he can protect Andrej from hundreds of miles away.

They meet in secret every few months — always brief, always careful.
They fuck like they're drowning — desperate, possessive, angry at the distance, angry at the pact, angry at everything except each other.
They never talk about the details of what happens in the gaps.
But they do talk about the future:
“When I’m free, I’m coming for you.”
“When I have enough leverage, I’ll burn the whole board down so we can walk away.”
2. **Middle phase (years 2–4): sharpening the blade**
Andrej outgrows the submissive role without ever openly rebelling.
He becomes Pontodoros’s right hand — the enforcer, the champion, the man who walks into rooms and makes people flinch without saying a word.
Pontodoros starts to rely on him more than he ever intended.
Andrej learns the political game from the inside — who owes who, who can be bought, who needs to be scared. He never uses it against Pontodoros (yet), but he files every detail away.

Rimmon rises fast.
He’s no longer just a clerk. He’s the shadow advisor to half a dozen powerful families. He brokers deals, sinks rivals, collects secrets like currency.
He builds alliances with people who hate Pontodoros — quietly, invisibly.
He becomes the man who can make a senator disappear without anyone ever proving it was murder.

They still meet when they can.
The sex becomes less desperate, more deliberate — they’re learning each other’s new bodies, new scars, new silences.
They still don’t talk about the gaps.
But the pact holds.
And the shared hatred of the situation starts to turn outward — toward the system that forced them into these roles, toward Pontodoros, toward the Empire that hunts people like them.
3. **Turning point & dark triumph (age 19–20 for Andrej)**
Andrej “graduates” from erōmenos to equal — not because Pontodoros lets him, but because Andrej simply stops performing submission.
He walks into Pontodoros’s villa one night, lays his spear on the table, and says:
“I’ve learned everything you can teach. The debt is paid. I’m done.”

Pontodoros smiles — not angry, almost proud.
“You think you can just leave?”

Andrej doesn’t answer with words.
He answers with presence: the man who can kill with a spear, navigate a ship through a storm, read a battlefield like a book, and walk away from any fight without a scratch.
Pontodoros sees it.
He lets Andrej go.
Not out of kindness — out of self-preservation.

Andrej goes straight to Rimmon.

Rimmon has been waiting.
He’s no longer a clerk. He’s the man who controls half the Senate’s secrets and a third of the trade council’s purse strings.
He has leverage.
He has power.
He has spent years building a net that can catch even Pontodoros.

They don’t run.
They don’t hide.
They **return** — to Mergos, to the heart of the city that tried to break them.

Andrej becomes Rimmon’s bodyguard — publicly.
Officially.
The silver-eyed warrior at the side of the rising political fixer.
The man who can kill with a glance, who never speaks unless necessary, who makes people remember why they should be afraid.

Rimmon becomes the power behind several thrones — the one who can ruin you with a whisper, who can make your enemies disappear, who can shift entire trade routes with a single letter.

They destroy their common enemies — quietly, ruthlessly, elegantly.
Pontodoros is the last.
They don’t kill him.
They simply make him irrelevant.
A retired veteran with no leverage left, watching from the sidelines as the boys he tried to own now own the board.

The cracks remain — they always will.
They still don’t talk about the gaps.
They still wake up sometimes reaching for each other and remembering nights they can’t share.
But the shared burden has made them stronger than any mentor or master could have forged them alone.

They are not innocent anymore.
They are not pure.
They are survivors who turned survival into supremacy.

And when they finally share a bed again — no roles, no performance, no debt — it’s not gentle because they’ve forgotten how to be rough.
It’s gentle because they’ve finally learned they don’t have to prove anything anymore.

They just have to be.

Together.

Unbreakable.

Unbowed.

Victorious.


🖤


That’s the ending I see now: not tragic redemption, not clean healing — a **dark, earned, survivor’s triumph**.
They don’t get the innocent love story back.
They get something harder, sharper, more unbreakable.


They get each other — scarred, powerful, and finally free to choose what they want instead of what they need to survive.


Do you want to see a short scene of that final confrontation with Pontodoros?
Or the moment Andrej steps into his role as Rimmon’s bodyguard in public for the first time?
Or the quiet bed scene years later, when they finally don’t need to prove anything?


I’m ready to write whichever piece you want next. 😈
